<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of Christ’s suffering, transitioning from his general role as a savior to the specific nature of his internal and external torments throughout his life.</meta>

Of the painful torment of Christ

## The 67th Objection <note>The term "Gegenwurff" (objection/counter-argument) suggests this text follows a scholastic format of questions and answers regarding faith.</note>

**How our Lord tormented and disciplined himself for our sake, and of his internal suffering.**

He who so graciously forgives our manifold and great debts, and who, out of the compassion he had for us regarding the pain we should have suffered according to divine justice, tormented and disciplined himself and was scourged in many ways all the days of his life. Before his agonizing crucifixion, he was wretchedly beaten and torn with sharp scourges. That he was manifoldly tormented throughout his entire life is frequently mentioned, as it stands written in many places of the Scriptures in the person of Christ, such as in the 30th, 37th, 68th, 88th, and 71st Psalms. <note>The author uses the Vulgate numbering for the Psalms, which often differs by one digit from modern Protestant Bibles.</note> In the 34th Psalm, he speaks: As one weeping and saddened, so was I brought low, and they rejoiced against me.

**Gloss.** <note>A "Glosa" or gloss is a standard medieval commentary on a biblical text.</note> I was sad; they were joyful. I mourned mercifully over their ruin and loss, but they rejoiced in my misfortunes and in my persecution. They have gathered together; the scourges are collected or heaped upon me.

**In the 30th Psalm:** For my life is consumed in pain and my years in sighing.

**In the 72nd Psalm:** I have washed my hands among the innocent and have been scourged the whole day, and my discipline <term>kestygūg</term> <note>This refers to ascetic self-discipline or punishment meant to purify.</note> was in the early morning and at the time of the midnight office.

By "the whole day," understand the entire life of our Lord, according to his own interpretation; for he himself said in the ninth chapter of John: I must work the works of Him who sent me while it is day. The night comes, when no one can work. And which "day" he meant, he clarified afterward when he said: As long as I am in the world, I am the light of the world. Do you see? As long as the Lord was in the world, that was the "day," and during that same entire day, the Lord was scourged—that is, tormented, saddened, troubled, and burdened by the constant carrying of our pains.

Beyond this, at the end, before the hard death of the cross, his entire tender body was torn with hard, fierce scourges, as if it were too little that he should die the bitter death of the cross, but that he should first be so wretchedly martyred in every limb.

¶ Regarding what the Lord suffered beyond the common pain of men, this short writing does not permit setting it down specifically, but only in a general way. Note that the Lord, beyond the common pain of men, had to suffer both physically and spiritually. Physically: such as hunger and thirst, heat and frost, fatigue and weakness. Spiritically: such as sadness, fear, shame before repentance, compassion, mercy, and the like. He had to suffer special pain and sorrow in two ways: namely, as if he were a sinful person <note>That is, taking the legal place of a sinner before God's judgment.</note> and in the capacity of being the head of all mankind, and most especially the head of the believers. Therefore, all their suffering—most of all that which they would suffer for his sake—went to his heart as his own suffering; and the other suffering, which they would otherwise endure as his members <note>A reference to the "Body of Christ," where believers are considered limbs or parts of Christ's own body.</note>, affected him too. And this was so much more than what they felt themselves, because he loved them more dearly and knew them more clearly than they knew themselves. Thus the Lord had these countless pains and sufferings within his own person; not that one should understand that suffering [existed] in his divine... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely continuing on the next page to clarify that Christ suffered in his human nature, not his divine nature.</note>

<summary>This page argues that Christ’s suffering was not limited to the crucifixion but spanned his entire life, interpreting various Psalms as prophetic evidence of his lifelong torment. It explains that as the "Head of Humanity," Christ felt the collective suffering of all believers even more intensely than they did themselves.</summary>

<keywords>Christ's Passion, Scourging, Psalms, Gloss, Internal Suffering, Spiritual Pain, Body of Christ, Asceticism, Divine Justice</keywords>

Of the painful torment of Christ

### The 67th Objection <note>A "Gegenwurff" is an objection or argument used in scholastic teaching to explore a theological question.</note>

How our Lord tormented and disciplined himself for our sake, and concerning his inner suffering.

He who so graciously forgives our manifold and great debts, and who, out of the compassion he had for us regarding the pain we should have suffered according to divine justice, tormented and disciplined himself and was scourged in many ways all the days of his life. Before his agonizing crucifixion, he was wretchedly beaten and torn with sharp scourges. That he was manifoldly tormented throughout his entire life is frequently mentioned, as it stands written in many places of the Scriptures in the person of Christ, such as in the 30th, 34th, 67th, 88th, and 66th Psalms. <note>The author refers to the Vulgate numbering of the Psalms. For example, Psalm 34 in the Vulgate is Psalm 35 in modern Bibles.</note> In the 34th Psalm, he himself speaks:

> As one weeping and as one saddened, so was I brought low; and they rejoiced against me.

<gloss>Gloss: <note>Standard medieval biblical commentary.</note> I was sad, they were joyful. I mourned mercifully over their ruin and loss, but they rejoiced in my misfortunes and in my persecution. They have gathered together; the scourges are collected or heaped upon me.</gloss> In the 30th Psalm: For my life is consumed in pain, and my years in sighing. In the 72nd Psalm: I have washed my hands among the innocent and have been scourged the whole day, and my discipline <term>kestyguñg</term> <note>From "Kasteiung," meaning ascetic discipline, mortification, or punishment of the flesh.</note> was in the early morning or around the time of matins. By "the whole day," understand the entire life of our Lord, according to his own interpretation when he spoke in the ninth chapter of John:

> I must work the works of him who sent me while it is day. The night comes, when no one can work.

And which day he meant, he clarified afterward when he spoke:

> As long as I am in the world, I am the light of the world.

Do you see? As long as the Lord was in the world, just so long was the day; and throughout that same entire day, the Lord was scourged—that is, tormented, saddened, troubled, and burdened through the constant bearing of our pains. Beyond this, at the last, before the hard death of the cross, he was torn through his whole tender body with hard, fierce scourges, just as if it were too little that he should die the bitter death of the cross unless he were first so wretchedly martyred in every limb.

What the Lord suffered beyond the common pains of men, this short writing does not permit to be set down in detail, but only in a general way. Note that the Lord, beyond the common pain of men, had to suffer both bodily and spiritually. Bodily, such as hunger and thirst, heat and frost, weariness and frailty <term>blödigkeit</term> <note>Original: "blödigkeit" – in this context, it refers to physical weakness or infirmity rather than a lack of intelligence.</note>; spiritually, such as sadness, fear, shame for the sake of repentance <note>Original: "furschā für rew" – likely meaning the shame he felt on behalf of humanity's sins.</note>, compassion, mercy, and the like, involving special pain and suffering. He had to suffer this in two ways: first, as he was a "person of sin" <note>Meaning he took the place of a sinner to atone for humanity.</note>, and second, as he was the Head of all people, and most of all of the believers. Therefore, all their suffering—most especially what they would suffer for his sake—went to his heart as his own suffering, and also the other suffering that they would otherwise endure as the suffering of his members. And this was so much more than what they felt themselves, by as much as he loved them more and recognized it more clearly than they did themselves. Thus, the Lord had these innumerable sorrows and sufferings alongside the suffering of his own person. This is not to be understood as if the suffering was in his divine

<summary>This page argues that Christ's suffering was not limited to the crucifixion but lasted his entire life, interpreting various Psalms and the Gospel of John to show that he endured both physical and spiritual pain as the "Head" of humanity.</summary>

He who so graciously forgives our
manifold and great debts, and who, out of the compassion he had for us
regarding the pain we should have suffered according to divine
justice, tormented and disciplined himself and was

Before his agonizing crucifixion, he was
wretchedly beaten and torn with sharp scourges.
But that he was manifoldly tormented
throughout his entire life is frequently
mentioned, as is written in many
places of the Scriptures in the person of Christ,
such as in the 30th, 33rd, 67th, 108th,
and 21st Psalms. <note>The author uses the Vulgate numbering of the Psalms. For instance, Psalm 30 in the Vulgate is Psalm 31 in modern Bibles.</note> Read for yourself: in the 34th
Psalm he speaks:
As one weeping and as one saddened, so was I
humbled; and they have rejoiced
against me.

in my persecution. They have come together;
the scourges are collected or
heaped upon me. **In the

pain, and my years in sighing.

my hands among the innocent, and
have been scourged the whole day,

according to his own interpretation; for he
spoke himself in the ninth chapter of John:
"I must work the works of him who
sent me, while it is day.
The night comes, when no man can work."
And which day he meant, he explained
afterward when he spoke:
"As long as I am in the world,
I am the light of the world."
Do you see? As long as the Lord was in the world,
so long was the day; and during that
entire day, the Lord was scourged—that is,
tormented, saddened, troubled, and
burdened through the constant bearing of our pains.
Beyond this, at the last, before
the hard death of the cross,
he was torn through his whole tender body
with hard, fierce scourges,
just as if it were not enough
that he should die the bitter death of the cross,
unless he were first so
wretchedly martyred in every limb.

But what the
Lord suffered beyond the common pain of men,
this short writing does not permit
to be set down in detail, but only in
a general way. Note that the Lord,
beyond the common pain of men, had to suffer
physically and spiritually.
Physically, such as hunger and thirst,
heat and frost, weariness and frailty.
Spiritually, such as sadness, fear, foresight, <note>likely "fürschau," referring to the anticipation of future suffering</note>
remorse, pity, mercy, compassion,
and the like, [which] he had to endure
as pain and suffering in two ways.
That is, according to his being a "sin-representative" <note>original: "sund person." Refers to Christ taking on the legal and spiritual status of a sinner to pay their debt.</note>
and according to his being the head of all men,
and most especially of the faithful.
Therefore, all their suffering—most of all
that which they would suffer for his sake—
went to his heart as his own
suffering; and the other [suffering] that they otherwise
would suffer, [went to his heart] as the suffering of his own limbs.
And that so much more than to themselves,
insofar as he loved them more, and more clearly
perceived [it] than they themselves did.
Thus the Lord had
these innumerable sorrows and sufferings,
along with the suffering of his own person—not that
man should understand that the suffering in his divine...

<meta>Title page of the 1551 edition. The text is a mix of German and Latin, typical of early modern scientific works. This page introduces a compendium of alchemical distillation and health advice.</meta>

# The Heaven of the Philosophers. <note>original: "Coelum Philosophorum"</note>

## Concerning the secrets of Nature / that is / how one should distill <term>Aqua Vitae</term> <note>literally "water of life," referring here to concentrated alcoholic spirits or medicinal essences</note> not only from wine / but also from all metals / fruits / meat / eggs / roots / herbs / and from many other things / for the preservation of the health of the human body / A short, clear / and useful book / through the diligence of Philip Ulstadt of Nuremberg / compiled in Latin from Raymond <note>Referring to Raymond Lull, a 13th-century polymath and influential figure in alchemy</note> / Arnold of Villanova / and Albertus Magnus / but now newly translated into German / and the chapters / which were previously missing / have been added here.

## Also Marsilio Ficino’s Rule of Life / regarding eating / drinking / housing / etc. Through which, with the help of God, his life was strengthened / and kept in health / until he reached the age of 116 years / he was still living in Italy in the year 1482. Along with many good recipes for all kinds of diseases / a cheap and very useful pharmacy for the common man.

->Two woodcut illustrations of alchemical furnaces for distillation. On the left is a round, brick-like furnace with flames and smoke escaping from several vents and tubes around its circumference and at the top. On the right is a larger, rectangular brick furnace with several openings and smoke rising from the top.<-

### Printed at Frankfurt am Main / by Hermann Gülfferich / in the Schnurgasse at the sign of the Pitcher.

### 1551.
<insert>1556</insert>

<summary>This title page introduces a foundational alchemical and medical handbook by Philip Ulstadt, which synthesizes the works of famous medieval authorities on the distillation of medicinal essences. It also includes a health regimen by the Italian Renaissance scholar Marsilio Ficino, presented as a practical guide for the common person's health.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, distillation, Aqua Vitae, Philip Ulstadt, Marsilio Ficino, Raymond Lull, Arnold of Villanova, Albertus Magnus, pharmacy, Frankfurt, 16th century</keywords>

<meta>catchword: Books</meta>

# To the rigorous and noble
## Felix von G. of Valenz, Lord, etc.
### Philippus Ulstadius sends his greetings.

Your well-being would be a particular pleasure to me, for I find myself very well by the grace of God, my most blessed Felix. I have often and much considered how I might show gratitude to you for your countless good deeds shown to me, as I well know that no vice is to be more firmly condemned by anyone than ingratitude. 

Therefore, I have deliberated over this humble gift, however small it is, yet gathered with my best possible diligence from many teachings through manifold reading and industrious research, to dedicate it to you. I am also without doubt that you will receive and accept this joyfully and with good intention. Although it is quite concisely and briefly composed, I do not doubt it will bring you some pastime and pleasure. 

This is partly because it is like a container or shrine <note>original: "schreinn"; a cabinet or casket for valuables</note> of the old and new medicines or remedies. Also, it is because these same secrets were described by Johannes de Rupescissa, Raymond Lully, Arnold of Villanova, and Albertus Magnus <note>These four figures were considered the foundational authorities of medieval alchemy and "distillation" chemistry.</note>—the highest researchers of all the hidden mysteries of Nature—yet they were written so obscurely by these philosophers, both in the fields of Medicine and Alchemy, that few or almost no one in our present times has been able to understand them. I consider this difficulty to have arisen from the fact that they [used] the teachings of the ancients or...

<summary>In this preface, Philippus Ulstadius dedicates his work to his patron, Felix von G., explaining that he has synthesized the notoriously difficult and obscure writings of famous medieval alchemists into a more accessible "shrine" of medical knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Philippus Ulstadius, Felix von G., Johannes de Rupescissa, Raymond Lully, Arnold of Villanova, Albertus Magnus, alchemy, medicine, gratitude, nature's secrets</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the dedication by Philippus Ulstadius to Felix von G., transitioning from the mention of ancient authorities (Lully, Rupescissa) to the specific challenges of publishing these "secret" texts and the expected criticism from both the elite and the unlearned.</meta>

Books of <term>Raymond Lully</term> <note>Latin: "Raymundi"; refers to Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher whose name was later attached to many influential alchemical texts.</note>—namely the *Book of Mercuries* <note>Latin: "Mercuriorum"</note>,
the *Testament* <note>Latin: "Testamentum"</note>, or the *Codicil* <note>Latin: "Codicillus"</note>, or others 
of the same kind—have come to hand or into sight, 
which we can even now only with difficulty 
obtain, even if we desire them. Although it 
is beyond doubt and well known that some 
still possess very good books, which are nonetheless 
despised by those inexperienced in natural things 
and held to be mere old wives' tales <note>original: "dantmär"; a derogatory term for trifles or nonsense stories.</note>. 
Therefore, I am mostly prompted—and not 
unreasonably—to fear that 
the fruit or honor of my labor will also be mocked, 
just as happened to that highly experienced master 
of this Art, <term>Raymond Lully</term>. Therefore, since 
such an excellent teacher did not lack <term>Zoiluses</term> <note>Latin: "Zoili"; after Zoilus, a Greek grammarian known for his bitter, carping criticism of Homer. It refers here to harsh, envious critics.</note> 
or envious spit-boxes, I can easily assume 
that they will not spare my book or myself with 
their treacherous and envious tongues either. 
For it will greatly displease some highly learned men 
of <term>Philosophy</term> who pursue this Art that 
a work, kept hidden by so many until now, 
should now be brought to light and 
made common to everyone. Conversely, 
the unlearned will scold without ceasing—
not out of envy for my work, but 
because they cannot properly understand it. 
Therefore, my most beloved <term>Felix</term>, take 
this my work or labor under 
your protection; it has come forth not for the sake 
of fame or praise, but for the common good. 
Live blessedly, an ornament of the Princes of the 
<term>Dauphiné</term> <note>original: "Delphinats"; a historical province in southeastern France.</note>, and keep 
me hereafter, as always, in your favor. 
From <term>Fribourg</term> in the <term>Swiss Confederacy</term> <note>original: "Eidgnoschafft"</note>, 
on the Monday after the day of the holy 
knight <term>St. George</term> <note>April 23rd.</note>. In the year after 
->the birth of Christ, 1525.<-

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, Ulstadius expresses his fear of criticism from both elitist "philosophers" who want to keep knowledge secret and the ignorant who cannot understand it. He dedicates his work to his patron, Felix, and signs off from Fribourg in 1525.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page follows the dedication where Ulstadius expressed his fear of criticism from both the elite and the unlearned. He now provides a glossary of terms to make the subsequent alchemical and medicinal instructions accessible to a wider audience.</meta>

# Understanding and Explanation of Several Words Used Here in This Book.

It should be noted that, as this little book will teach hereafter, the true art of **Distillation**—especially concerning **Potable Gold** <note>Latin: *Aurum potabile*; a medicinal solution of gold thought to have life-prolonging properties.</note> and the **Fifth Essence** <note>Latin: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest part of a substance, separate from the four elements.</note>, which people have pursued for many a year and day—is indicated here in various ways. You may choose whichever of these ways pleases you and work therein. Do not let yourself be vexed by the effort, for it will well reward you in the use of medicine and many other good items, which are so artfully made and useful to mankind.

Furthermore, for the benefit of the common people to understand certain words, a portion is also shown here which serves that purpose.

Furthermore, what **Digestion** <note>German: *Digerieren*.</note> is: this is to soak a thing.
**Putrefaction**: this is to decay or to curdle. **Birthwort**: <term>Aristolochia</term> <note>German: *Holawurz*; a medicinal plant.</note>.
**Digestion**: this is the process of breaking down. **Amalgam**: this is a type of paste.
**Dissolving**: this is the breaking apart of other things. **Cementing**: this is to separate <note>In alchemy, a dry process for purifying gold.</note>.
**Coagulation**: this is a running together. **Dregs**: these are the sediment <note>Latin: *Feces*; German: *Drusen*.</note>.
**Fermentation**: this is a mixing or putting together.
**Vegetables**: these are things that dissolve other things <note>The text defines *Vegetabilia* here in an alchemical-functional sense rather than just "plants."</note>.
**Fixed**: this is to make a thing stable or prepared. **Salt of Niter**: this is saltpeter.
**Imbibing**: to mix thoroughly together. **Rhubarb**: <note>The text uses *Rauedseni*, a term derived from Persian for medicinal rhubarb.</note>.
**Diet**: a good regimen, as in during an illness.
**Great Theriac**: the best of <term>theriacs</term> <note>A famous medicinal "cure-all" compound containing many ingredients.</note>. **Laserwort**: a seed <note>Latin: *Siler montanus*.</note>.
**Theriac of Andromachus**: theriac from Alexandria. **Bistort**: <term>Tormentil</term> <note>Medicinal plants used as astringents.</note>.
**Mercury**: quicksilver. **Camel Grass**: <note>Latin: *Schoenanthus*; German: *Kamelthier hew*. An aromatic grass used in ancient medicine.</note>.
**Long Pepper**: these are round peppercorns. **Nasturtium**: watercress.
**Prepared Coriander**: processed coriander. **Tincture**: a color.
**White Agaric**: white larch mushrooms. **Select**: a choice or exquisite thing.
**Phlegm**: bad moisture or slime that sticks.
**Socotrine Aloe**: a juice named as such. **Nux Vomica**: <term>Crow's eye</term> <note>German: *Kronånglin*. A seed containing strychnine, used in small doses in early medicine.</note>.
**Musk**: fresh, good musk. **Spodium**: burnt ivory.
**French Lavender**: flowers named as such <note>Latin: *Stoechas*.</note>. **Ivory Shavings**: scraped ivory.
**Rind**: the skin or peel of any thing. **Dia-Margariton**, **Dia-Ambra**, etc.
These are all **Confections**, which are found in the apothecary shops.

Whatever further need you have for certain words may be noted down, and what is necessary may be taken from the apothecary or ordered to be prepared.

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page serves as the "Preface to the Reader," following the index. It transitions from the organizational table of contents to the author's justification for the work, emphasizing clarity over the obscured texts of previous alchemists. Catchword: "Das" (The).</meta>

# Preface to the Reader.

Indeed, there is no one who does not know that the ancient teachers and writers produced many highly famous and beautiful books concerning the Fifth Essence <note>Latin: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest, most concentrated medicinal spirit extracted from a substance, believed to be the "fifth element" beyond earth, air, fire, and water.</note>, the Golden Drink <note>German: *gülden Tranck*; likely referring to *Aurum Potabile* or drinkable gold, a legendary alchemical medicine thought to grant longevity.</note>, and also the Water of Life <note>German: *wasser des Lebens*; *Aqua Vitae*, usually a high-proof distilled spirit used as a solvent or medicine.</note>. We see the same being done by their successors. However, because all their writings were issued in such a mutilated, shortened, and obscured manner—and some previous books were such that even a very diligent and skillful student of this Art was led astray more than helped or guided onto the right path toward fulfilling his desires—we could not refrain from providing a more certain written instruction. In this work, both a thorough understanding and the practical labor of this Art shall be shown, through which we may easily attain our desire and bring our undertaking to a good end. We freely admit that we have written this book somewhat prematurely or hastily, and in simple, unadorned speech; one should consider the reason for this. Partly, it is so that the Art is no longer withheld from those workers who are currently wandering in error; and furthermore, so that inexperienced beginners might understand it all the better. Therefore, let this general rule be our beginning.

### A General Rule.

The "Heaven of the Sages" or the Philosophical Heaven <note>The "Philosophical Heaven" usually refers to a specific type of alchemical vessel (like a circulatory flask) or a highly refined state of matter that can capture "celestial" influences.</note> perfectly draws into itself the power or influence of the stars, regardless of their temperament, nature, or properties. As indeed Raymond Lully, Arnold of Villanova, and John of Rupescissa <note>Three of the most influential medieval alchemists; their works focused heavily on distillation and the "Fifth Essence."</note> point out.

<image-desc>A large, elaborate calligraphic knot-work design consisting of multiple interlocking circles and loops, creating a symmetrical decorative ornament at the bottom of the page.</image-desc>

<summary>The author introduces the work as a practical and clear guide to alchemy, specifically the Fifth Essence and Aqua Vitae, aiming to correct the intentionally confusing writings of earlier authorities. He cites famous alchemists like Lully and Villanova to establish the foundational "General Rule" regarding the Philosophical Heaven.</summary>

<keywords>Preface, Fifth Essence, Alchemy, Water of Life, Potable Gold, Raymond Lully, Arnold of Villanova, John of Rupescissa, Philosophical Heaven, distillation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the main body of the text, following the preface. It defines the central concept of the work—the "Fifth Essence"—and establishes the philosophical and theological boundaries of alchemical medicine. Catchword: "or print-".</meta>

# Of the Secrets of Nature
## that is / How one should make various Waters of Life / a useful book / by Philippus Ulstadius.

---

## The First Chapter
### What the Fifth Essence / or the fifth being is / of every single substance / and for what the same is useful.

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial 'D' featuring intricate vine-work and scroll patterns within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>

Therefore, it is necessary first of all to know what the Fifth Essence <note>Latin: *Quinta Essentia*; in medieval alchemy, this was the "purest" part of a substance, separate from the four elements of earth, air, fire, and water.</note>—or in German, the "fifth being"—of every single thing or matter is. If this is recognized, all other things will be easily understood; for (according to the saying of Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, a Roman statesman and philosopher whose works on rhetoric and ethics were foundational to Renaissance education.</note>) all teaching and instruction of any subject should proceed from a definition, so that it may be known what one intends to speak about.

<margin>Description of the Fifth Essence.</margin>
Therefore, you should know that the Fifth Essence is the fifth being of every thing that has a form and shape; it is the most delicate Soul [drawn] from its Body—that is, from the coarse Matter and the superfluity of the four elements—extracted through the most skillful and subtle Distillation. We shall teach this hereafter and intend to provide diligent instruction on how it should be done. Furthermore, no one should wonder why this Art was hidden from people for so long; for many of the ancient Philosophers <note>Latin: *Philosophi*</note> labored and worked in this Art to sustain human life and preserve it for as long as possible, delaying the arrival of death and reaching the naturally appointed end. Yet, they did not intend to extend that life beyond the goal set by God—as some useless, frivolous, and careless people might dare to claim or promise—for as Paul <note>The Apostle Paul, referring to Hebrews 9:27.</note> also indicates: That all men must die once. Therefore, while it is against Nature to maintain life eternally indestructible through any kind of destructible medicine, Nature is yet powerful enough to preserve and keep that life uninjured through every age by means of a medicine that is almost equal to the indestructible.

<margin>Nature can, with the medicine of the Fifth Essence, keep the age of man uninjured.</margin>
To ensure this happens in the best possible way, the very best Antidote <note>Latin: *Antidotum*; here used in the sense of a preventative or restorative medicine rather than just a cure for poison.</note>—that is, a preventative medicine—is the Fifth Essence. Since it is not an elemental thing, but rather like a soul separated from its body, it possesses no quality or nature, being neither cold nor warm, moist <note>The text ends mid-sentence, likely continuing with "nor dry," referring to the four primary qualities of Aristotelian physics.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 1, defining the "Fifth Essence" as the refined "soul" of a substance extracted through distillation. It argues that while medicine cannot grant immortality against God's will, it can preserve health and prolong life to its natural limit.</summary>
<keywords>Fifth Essence, distillation, longevity, alchemy, nature of matter, Cicero, Apostle Paul, medicine, quintessence</keywords>

It transitions from the general theory of its nature to the practical observation that it can be extracted from many substances, but most excellently from wine.</meta>

from wine / yet there are still many other materials / from which one may extract the Fifth Essence <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*; in alchemy, the "fifth essence" or quintessence was the purest, most concentrated part of a substance, beyond the four physical elements.</note> / such as from all metals / from all fruits / from meat / eggs / from roots / herbs / and many other diverse things / as will be taught hereafter.

It is also called by others the *Water of Life* <note>original: *Aqua Vite*</note> / that is / a water of life / because it protects human life

## The Second Chapter / How one extracts the Fifth Essence / through a Distilling Apparatus.

## II

### Of the Secrets of Nature /

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two glass alchemical vessels: a flask (cucurbit) and a retort with a long, curved neck. To the right of the vessels is a large, ornate decorative initial letter 'S'.</image-desc>

**S**ince one has now recognized / or understood / what the Fifth Essence <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*</note> is / and for what it serves / or why it was discovered / it is to be considered / through how many various ways / and from which things / one might extract it / And since it is extracted from moist / dry / hot / and cold things / we wish first <margin>Fifth Essence from wine.</margin> to say / how one extracts it from moist things / such as from wine / and that occurs as follows.

Take of the very best red wine / which is a little sweet / and in no way mixed or adulterated / but simply natural / not too new / nor too old / but of a middle age / Or if you cannot obtain such red wine / then take the very best white wine / as much as you desire. Put it into a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass flask used in distillation</note> / up to two parts full / and the third part empty / Afterward place a narrow-beaked <term>alembic</term> <note>the cap or "still-head" that sits atop the flask to collect and direct vapors</note> upon it / in such a way / that the beak goes into the upper part of the receiver glass. Afterward make everything tight / with a lute <note>a paste used to seal joints in alchemical apparatus</note> made of paper / that has been soaked for a long time / Or a mixture of flour / and egg white / as thick as honey. It may also, according to the opinion of <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a famous Majorcan philosopher and mystic often associated with alchemical works</note> Raymond Lull / be made / with white frankincense. And with such lute / shall the mentioned three vessels / be luted / or sealed up / so that the lively / and increasing powers do not evaporate / from the matter / which one intends to distill / therefore the glasses should be luted thus / as is shown here.

When that has now happened / then place the cucurbit into a ring / which has many holes / which is called <term>Dripes Arconorum</term> <note>a Latinized corruption, likely meaning "Tripod of Secrets"</note> / that is / a tripod / of secrets / or hidden things.

Afterward set the tripod into a furnace / which has a wide cauldron / such as the <term>Balneum Mariae</term> <note>literally "Mary's Bath"; a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat</note> is / in such a way that the cauldron is half / or two-thirds full of water. And you should place the tripod in the same cauldron / and the same cauldron should /

<meta>This page continues the practical instructions for distillation, shifting from the preparation of the vessel and wine to the precise management of heat. It introduces the four degrees of fire, a fundamental concept in alchemical practice, and warns against the dangers of excessive heat and the incompetence of untrained practitioners.</meta>

# Philippus Ulstadius. III

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a distillation apparatus consisting of a brick furnace base, a cucurbit (vessel) inside, and an alembic (head) with a long delivery tube leading to a receiver flask. The apparatus is equipped with side handles or weights.</image-desc>

<margin>Of the degrees of fire.</margin>
Galen and Raymond <note>likely referring to the Greek physician Galen and the alchemist Raymond Lull</note> say: Things can suffer nothing violent without the destruction of their original essence. For (as Avicenna <note>a Persian polymath and influential physician</note> testifies) there are four degrees of heat, just as there are four <term>complexions</term> <note>the balance of the four humors or qualities in a substance</note> or qualities. Of these, the first degree is lukewarm, like a water tempered in such a way that it does not burn if one held a finger or hand in it. The second degree is tolerably hot, such that it is not very harmful to any limb placed within it. The third degree is nearly boiling hot, unbearable and not without injury to a limb that one might put in it. The fourth degree, however, is of such swift heat that one may in no way endure it, being entirely boiling and exceedingly hot. Some call the fire of the first degree a "fire of the bath" <note>original: "fewr des Bads"; a *balneum Mariae* or water bath</note>. The fire of the second degree, a "fire of the ashes." A fire of the third degree, a "burning fire." The fourth degree they reject. But according to the opinion of some other craftsmen, the fire of the bath is in the first degree. The fire of the ashes in the second degree. The fire of the sand <note>a sand bath, which provides more intense, even heat than water</note> in the third degree. If it were necessary to go as far as the fourth degree (which however rarely happens), one should take in place of sand <term>Limaturam Martis</term> <note>original Latin: "iron filings"; in alchemy, Mars is the symbol for iron</note>. This becomes a fire of exceeding heat. And for this one shall coat the <term>Cucurbita</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass flask</note> all around with a "lute" or clay, as one does when one separates or distills <term>Aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "Aqua fort"; nitric acid, literally "strong water," used to dissolve metals</note>, so that it does not break from the excessive heat, since the iron filings become white-hot. And through these last four degrees you may govern the fire in the very best way, whereas according to your Avicenna, you can hardly or only with great effort do so.

<margin>One should never heat the fire to the last degree during distillation.</margin>
Herewith I wish to have warned every lover or diligent student of this art to take note: that in any and every distillation, he should never increase the fire to the fourth degree, for it would be too violent for the matter one wished to distill, so that it could not endure it, and subsequently would be ruined by such overwhelming heat. And for that reason, all artists or craftsmen reject this fourth degree.

<margin>Hedge-apothecaries should distill no waters.</margin>
For this reason, there are certain druggists, apothecaries, and others of that kind, to whom the art of distillation is wrongly permitted, even though it belongs to the philosophers and physicians, who are the researchers of nature. For nature is so ordained by the Almighty and Gracious God that she can suffer nothing violent without her own destruction, as one may prove from the opinions of many philosophers. Therefore, I advise the lovers of this art that they should in no way undertake this highly venerable art—granted to us by Divine Providence for the maintenance of human life—unless they have perfect knowledge of these four degrees and the same...

<sig>A iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>This page explains the four degrees of heat used in distillation, ranging from a gentle water bath to intense iron-filing heat, while warning that the highest degree often ruins the substance. Ulstadius also cautions that distillation is a philosophical and medical science that should not be practiced by untrained "hedge-apothecaries."</summary>

<keywords>distillation, four degrees of fire, Avicenna, Galen, alchemical furnace, iron filings, cucurbit, aqua fortis, apothecary, philosophy of nature</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical instructions for distillation, shifting from the general definitions of heat to the specific requirements for each of the seven stages of distillation. It emphasizes the need for a gradual reduction in temperature as the substance becomes increasingly refined.</meta>

<header>On the Secrets of Nature</header>

<margin>Fire of the first distillation.</margin>
One must know how to govern and temper the fire in all things and at all times, as is proper. Therefore, in the First Distillation, one should make the fire with small coals or with very dry wood, up to the end of the third degree of heat, so that the <term>balneum</term> <note>original: "Balneum"; a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat</note> or <margin>2nd</margin> bath almost boils. In the second distillation, however, you should let up and soften the fire by one or two-thirds of a degree, <margin>3rd</margin> and almost down to one degree, so that it has come down nearly to the second degree. But in the third distillation, you should constantly and gradually let up and soften the fire until it reaches the <margin>4th</margin> second degree and one-third of a degree. In the fourth, it should decrease to the second degree. In the fifth, it should <margin>5th</margin> come down to one degree and two-thirds of a degree. In the sixth, it should decrease to one degree and one <margin>6th</margin> quarter of a degree. In the seventh and final <margin>7th</margin> distillation, however, one should soften the fire so that it is brought to the first degree—that is, so that it is lukewarm—and one should not descend any further.

And note that every degree has three intervals or stages: namely, the Beginning, the Middle, and the End. This is to be understood as follows: the reason one constantly reduces the degrees of heat is that when the matter is in the First Distillation, it is entirely coarse and raw, such that it does not allow itself to be distilled easily because of its impure substance. And for this reason, it is not like the others—such as the second, third, or subsequent distillations. Therefore, in the first distillation, one should increase the fire from the first up to the third degree, so that it reaches the final third of that same third degree, making the <term>balneum</term> <note>original: "Balneum"; Latin for bath</note> as hot as possible—the <margin>Bath.</margin> bath being the water in which you distill—yet it should not boil.

In the second distillation, one should proceed more gently, as the matter has yielded somewhat through the first stage and has been refined; therefore, it does not require such a great fire. And in the third, it is even more refined. And thus, you should constantly and gradually descend in every distillation and soften the fire, for (as has often been said) if you force the substance beyond measure for distillation...

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a chemical furnace, specifically a "lazy Heinz" or athanor. It is a cylindrical brick structure with a small fuel door at the bottom. On the flat top of the furnace sit several distillation vessels (cucurbits with alembic heads) of various sizes, indicating simultaneous distillations. One vessel is larger and centrally placed, surrounded by smaller ones.</image-desc>

<margin>In the tempering of the fire lies all the art of distilling.</margin>
<margin>Lazy Furnace. <note>original: "Furnus Accidiosus"; a furnace designed to burn for long periods with minimal attention.</note></margin>
...its nature will be destroyed or corrupted. For Galen and Aristotle <note>the famous Greek physician and philosopher, respectively</note> say: nature can suffer nothing from violent motion. And therefore, one must take care with all possible understanding so that you do not fail or err in the degrees of heat, but rather moderate them, for therein lies the true art of all distillation. For this reason, we have wished to explain the entire substance of the matter openly, so that no one might think or suspect that we intended to mix honey and wormwood <note>a metaphor for mixing something sweet with something bitter or deceptive</note>. This little figure, which you see depicted here, is the form of the "Lazy Heinz" <note>original: "faulen Heinzen"; a common alchemical term for an athanor furnace, named for its self-feeding fuel hopper which required less "work" from the operator</note>.

<summary>This page provides a precise schedule for reducing heat across seven successive distillations, explaining that as matter becomes more refined, it requires less energy. It also introduces the "Lazy Heinz" or athanor furnace as the ideal tool for maintaining these delicate temperatures.</summary>
<keywords>distillation, seven stages, degrees of heat, balneum, water bath, tempering fire, Lazy Heinz, athanor, Galen, Aristotle, Furnus Accidiosus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for alchemy, following the discussion of heat levels. It introduces the concept of "Putrefaction"—a process of rotting or breaking down matter—which prepares the substance for the seven stages of distillation described on the previous page.</meta>

->Philippus Ulstadius<-

## The Third Chapter: How one should 
## putrefy the raw and unrefined 
## matter for 
## distillation.

It is to be observed with all diligence what kind of substance the matter is that one wishes to <term>digest</term> <note>original: "Digerieren"; a process of exposing a substance to gentle, steady heat over a long period</note>—namely, whether it is of a hard or soft, that is to say, a coarse or subtle substance—and how and through what means one may <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: "Putrificiern"; in alchemy, this refers to a controlled decomposition intended to "open" the substance and release its essence</note> and digest it, so that it may be distilled all the more easily, and so that the pure may be separated from the impure, the coarse from the subtle, and vice versa. And therefore, whatever it may be from which you wish to extract the <term>Quinta Essentia</term> <note>the "Fifth Essence" or quintessence; the purest, most medicinal part of a substance</note> through distillation, you shall, first and before all things, putrefy it in horse manure which is warm of its own nature and not made warm by any other accidental means. You shall digest it there for two months. And again, between the first and second distillation, for one month; between the second and third, for three weeks; between the third and fourth, for fifteen days; between the fourth and fifth, for eight days; between the fifth and sixth, for four days; and between the sixth and seventh, for two days. And know that the manure must always be at a steady warmth; for if the warmth fails, the circulation of the water would be destroyed, and subsequently the matter which was to be brought to a <term>Quinta Essentia</term> would be separated or divided in the <note>original: "wārme des Himmels"</note> heat of the heavens. You may perceive this by the double or middle dividing line which separates the <term>Quinta Essentia</term> (which is the upper part) from the <term>Secessen</term> <note>original: "Secessen"; from the Latin 'secessus', meaning the dregs, waste, or sediment</note>—namely, the bottom part. And here you should note that these degrees of digestion and putrefaction belong to their matters just as was said above regarding the degrees of heat. Therefore, a longer <term>putrefaction</term> is required before the distillation than after it. When the first distillation has occurred, the matter no longer has so much coarseness within it and is more fit to become a <term>Quinta Essentia</term> than before. Therefore, the putrefaction after the first distillation should happen in a shorter time than before, because the matter has become subtler and purer than it was previously. Thus, it is not unreasonable to note that seven digestions and putrefactions should take place, just as seven distillations must occur. The philosophers, according to their opinions, specify the way to digest as follows: that anyone who wishes to accomplish such a work should have a pit made, five feet deep and six feet wide, or perhaps a little more. And one should make it in a very damp place, such as in a cellar. When it is made, one shall place a <term>Stratum</term> <note>a layer</note>—that is, a layer or a spread—of unslaked lime upon the bottom, half a foot deep.

<summary>This chapter explains the process of "putrefaction" in horse manure, which prepares raw materials for distillation by breaking them down over specific, decreasing intervals of time. It provides instructions for building a digestion pit in a cellar using unslaked lime and manure to maintain steady heat.</summary>

# Of the Secrets of Nature /

<margin>**What method one should observe when one wishes to distill**</margin>
...place it high; upon which <term>stratum</term> <note>original: "Stratum"; a layer or bed of material</note> one shall make another of horse manure, which should not be too rotten, nor too fresh; upon that same stratum place the glass with the matter that you wish to <term>ferment</term> <note>original: "Fermentierē"; here referring to the chemical breakdown or preparation of the substance</note>, and cover it all around with such manure until the entire pit is full.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a chemical vessel, a cucurbit (Cucurbita), with a long neck, likely placed in a digestion pit as described in the text.</image-desc>

Afterward, moisten the manure with lukewarm water, according to how large and wide the pit is, until one feels the warmth on the glass <term>cucurbita</term> <note>original: "Cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped chemical flask used in distillation</note>. And this usually happens within half an hour; but if it does not happen immediately, moisten it further with the previously mentioned lukewarm water, and this should happen three or four times in a week; however, in such a way that one always removes the old <term>lime</term> <note>original: "Kälck"; quicklime, which generates heat when moistened</note> and manure and puts others there anew, and makes a new stratum, as was said before. And this way shall be maintained with all things that you wish to distill. And for this reason, <term>digestion</term> occurs, so that the coarse matter is gently <term>subtilized</term> <note>refined or made less dense</note> without any violent movement, action, or change, and is made ready for distillation.

Those things which are to be <term>digested</term> are arranged in various ways according to the four degrees of heat, so that in the first degree there is more work to be done than in the second, third, fourth, and the others, as was previously said regarding the warming of the <term>balneum</term> or bath; and therefore one should act more moderately in the second digestion.

<margin>**Seven ways to digest the matters.**</margin>
And the stratum of the lime should be thinner, and the stratum of the manure raised higher than in the first; thus you shall proceed in the digestions from now on until the last, where you shall make no stratum of lime, but only of manure alone.

<margin>.ij.</margin>
One may digest through this second way: that one places the cucurbit with the matter in the <term>Dog Days</term> <note>original: "Hundtstagen"; the hottest period of summer, typically July and August, associated with the rising of the Star Sirius</note> for a time in the sun to digest.

<margin>.iij.</margin>
Or one may also place the same matter for digestion upon a tiled stove or baking oven in the winter.

<margin>.iiij.</margin>
A <term>putrefaction</term> <note>the process of organic breakdown through gentle heat</note> may also occur with a steel mirror, such that one places the matter for digestion between the mirror and the sun, so that the rays of the sun strike against it, and from that same heat the digestion may occur in the Dog Days, as you see here in the figure.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing the sun with a face (Sol) emitting rays onto a circular mirror. The mirror reflects these concentrated rays onto a Cucurbita (flask) resting on a small pedestal, illustrating a method of solar digestion.</image-desc>

<margin>.v.</margin>
Or that in those same days one places the cucurbit in a vessel full of sand, and gradually warms it with warm, lukewarm water, and two-thirds of the cucurbit should be buried in the sand.

<margin>.vi</margin>
Or that one places the glass in a warm, lukewarm water, almost like a <term>Balneum Mariae</term> <note>original: "Balneum Mariæ"; Mary's Bath, or a double boiler, which provides gentle, indirect heat</note>, and that may occur in two ways;

B

The first method is to have a kettle placed in the neck of the furnace, ensuring the fire is not directly beneath it; otherwise, the natural substance would be destroyed by excess heat rather than being <term>rectified</term> <note>original: "gerechtfertigt"; here meaning purified or refined by the fire</note>. The second way is to place the glass containing the material into an anthill during the summer, so that its bottom is entirely buried while the sun strikes the upper part; in this way, you shall witness a wonderful <term>digestion</term> <note>original: "Digestion"; the process of softening and refining a substance through gentle, steady heat, often compared to the stomach's digestion</note>.

<margin>7</margin>
Seventh and finally, one may <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: "Putrificieren"; the alchemical breakdown of a substance using moist heat, like composting</note> the material in the month of October <note>original: "Weinmonat" (Wine month)</note> using the grape skins left over after the grapes have been pressed in the winepress. These skins possess no accidental or harsh heat, much like horse manure, and have a marvelous effect when one buries the circulation glasses within them. And although many only describe seven ways to perform digestion, nonetheless <margin>Isidore.</margin> Isidore <note>likely referring to Isidore of Seville, a famed medieval encyclopedist</note> says that regarding juniper wood, if one buries the coals in juniper ash and covers them very well, the fire may be kept throughout the entire year; in such ash, a very good digestion can take place. Let this be enough said concerning putrefaction and the materials for digestion.

## The Fourth Chapter / How one may recognize if a digested wine is fit to be distilled <term>Circulatorie</term> <note>original: "Circulatorie"; a method of distillation where the vapor rises, condenses, and flows back down into the same vessel in a continuous cycle</note>, or in a circular manner.

<margin>Whether the wine is sufficiently digested, the first proof.</margin>
The reason why wine, after being digested seven times in the prescribed manner, must be tested is so you may prove if it is fit to be distilled in a circle. Moisten a small linen cloth in the wine that has been so digested and light it with a candle. If the wine burns away without damaging the cloth, then it is not yet rectified; rather, it is a certain sign that some watery substance still remains within it. Therefore, one must always apply diligence to digest it as often and as much as needed until one achieves the proof. <margin>The second proof.</margin> Some say that one should put sugar into it, and if it is well rectified, both the wine and the sugar will burn away together when ignited. Therefore, you should note that this digested wine cannot yet be called the <term>quinta Essentia</term> <note>the "Fifth Essence" or most pure spiritual core of a substance</note>, but rather is "subject to the Quintessence." <margin>When the wine shall be called Quintessence.</margin> But when the linen cloth burns along with the wine, and the wine has lost all unpleasant, sharp, and sulfurous taste—so much so that it seems sweet when one tastes it, and no smoky residue remains from the burning—only then may one call it the <term>quintam Essentiam</term>. However, in things that cannot be proven by taste or smell, such as gold, silver, and other metals and precious stones from which one might extract a <margin>proof of Quintessence in metals.</margin> Quintessence (which belongs in the <term>Aurum potabile</term> <note>original: "Aurum potabile"; potable or drinkable gold, considered a universal medicine</note>), one must pay attention to the smoke. The vessels for circular distillation are described in various ways by many teachers. Some make a glass in a specific shape that is called a <term>blind alembic</term> <note>original: "Alembicum Cecum"; a still-head without a spout, forcing the vapors to drop back down</note>, so that one may place it on top, and they make...

# Of the Secrets of Nature

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of a pelican-style distillation vessel with two curved side arms looping from the top back into the body.</image-desc>
...at the top is a small hole through which one puts the <term>material</term> <note>original: "Matery"; the substance being distilled</note> in and out when it is to be <term>distilled circularly</term> <note>original: "Circkel weiß Distillieren"; a process where the substance evaporates and then flows back down into the base to be evaporated again, purifying it further each time</note>, or after it has been distilled. This is sealed with a glass shard made for that purpose, so that it can be closed off on all sides to prevent the material from evaporating away. One may also make the seal with silver, but in such a way that the hole is completely plugged. Some call this glass a **Pelican**, the form of which you see here.

One may also call these the **Two Brothers**. In this way, the material that one wishes to distill circulatory rises in one and descends in the other, and vice versa—always moving from one into the other, up and down.

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of a "blind alembic," consisting of two vessels stacked vertically, the top one being a spoutless head or helm.</image-desc>

This is done so that two cucurbits are placed or tucked one on top of the other, or into each other. The bottom one should have a wider mouth than the top one, and they must be <term>luted</term> <note>original: "verlutiert"; sealed together using a specialized airtight paste or clay</note> together in the best possible way, as seen here.

There are still others who make circulation glasses that are uniform from bottom to top, with a beak in the middle.

<meta>This page concludes the description of specialized glassware, specifically the "Pelican" or "Hermetic Vessel," and begins Chapter Five, which details the practical application of heat and the philosophical nature of the "Fifth Essence."</meta>

# Philippus Ulstadius

...some call it a **Pellicanum** <note>original: "Pellicanum"; a Pelican vessel</note>, or a **Pelican**, others a **Vas Hermetis** <note>Latin: "Vessel of Hermes"</note>. It is the most noble and convenient of all **circulating** <note>original: "Circulier"; referring to vessels designed for continuous reflux</note> glasses. However, they are rare to obtain because not all glassmakers have the skill to fashion them from a single piece. If a skilled craftsman comes to your hand from whom you can obtain such a vessel, you should spare neither gold nor silver to acquire it.

### The Fifth Chapter: How One Should Distill Circulatorily, or in Cycles.

Once you recognize through the aforementioned testing that the <term>material</term> <note>the substance being processed</note> has been sufficiently **digested** <note>original: "Digeriert"; a process of softening or preparing a substance using gentle heat</note> through manifold **putrefaction** <note>original: "Putrifaction"; the stage of breaking down or "rotting" the organic matter to release its essence</note>, and you have the proper glasses for **circulating**, then place the material into the said **circulatory vessel**. Set it in **horse manure** <note>original: "Roßmist"; decomposing manure provided a steady, gentle heat essential for alchemical processes</note>, or in the sun during the **Dog Days** <note>the hottest days of summer</note>, or in a **Balneum Mariae** <note>Latin: "Bath of Mary"; a water bath or bain-marie</note> that has been warmed. Keep it at these degrees of heat, or perhaps a little more, depending on how you see the material has been tempered and refined through the **digestion**. Leave it there for a long time until it is converted into a **Quinta Essentia** <note>Latin: "Fifth Essence"; the purest, most concentrated medicinal force of a substance</note> according to your desire.

You may recognize this transition by the taste, as was said before. Pay diligent attention: if something of the nature or sign of the original substance appears—perhaps as a murky little cloud at the bottom—you must immediately separate it through **distillation**. Once that is done, you should **circulate** it again as you began. Or, if it is more convenient, strain it into another circulatory vessel so that the coarse parts of the material remain behind in the first glass.

Therefore, when a diligent researcher of this Art prepares and attains the **Fifth Essence** through the refining and purification of the materials of wine or fruit, he should also believe that such a thing may occur in all other things. For this reason, the opinion of **Aristotle** is to be believed: that through **distillation**, a material thus ennobled and made non-material cannot henceforth be **reduced** <note>original: "Duciert"; brought back or led back</note> into a physical form or any kind of spiritual shape, provided it is without **elemental** substance and surpasses formability and destructibility.

Because of this, it is almost a **heavenly body**. For just as the heavens show themselves superior to all other things, so the **Fifth Essence** shows itself superior to all **complexions** <note>the balance of the four humors/qualities in a body</note> and qualities. And as all the stars follow their course from the most noble influence of the **First Mover** <note>a philosophical concept of the primary cause of all motion in the universe</note>, so also do all medicines with which the Fifth Essence is mixed draw their power from it—just as the **Adamant** <note>original: "Adamant"; here referring to a magnet or lodstone</note> draws iron to itself. But because this requires such great labor and one becomes so very weary of it, it is despised by everyone. From this it follows that in our modern times, one finds very few **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophos"; here meaning alchemists or natural philosophers</note> who are willing to undertake it.

## The Sixth Chapter
### What difference there is between Circulating and Distilling.

<image-desc>An illustration of a wide-mouthed cucurbit (flask) fitted with a blind helm (a lid without a spout), used for the alchemical process of digestion.</image-desc>

It is to be noted with great diligence what the difference is between **distillation** or **digestion** <note>original: "Digestion"; a process of slow heating to soften or mature a substance</note>. First, regarding the glasses that are required for this: it is necessary for digestion to have <term>cucurbits</term> <note>gourd-shaped flasks</note> and vessels that are wide at the top, so that one may put coarse things inside them. Upon these, one should set **Alembicos Cecos** <note>Latin: "Alembicos Cecos"; "blind" distilling heads</note>, which are "blind" lids without a spout, so that you may remove the coarse **material** <note>original: "Matery"</note> once it has been digested, take away the blind lid, and set a "beaked" <note>spouted</note> one upon it. 

If the material is pure once it has been distilled, and the **feces** <note>original: "Feces"; the dregs or solid residue remaining after distillation</note> do not mix with the distillate—as is the case with the **quinta Essentia** <note>Latin: "Fifth Essence"; the purest, most concentrated medicinal spirit</note> of wine—then you may take a **circulatorium** <note>a circulatory vessel</note> that is uniform at the top and bottom and of a wide width, but it should be narrow in the middle. At the beginning of the lower belly, it should have a tube a finger's length, but it should be of a smaller thickness. Yet there are some who make vessels for 

<image-desc>An illustration of a Pelican flask, a specialized alchemical vessel with two side-tubes that loop from the top back into the main body, used for the process of circulation.</image-desc>

**circulating**, which are called a **Pellican** <note>original: "Pellican"; named for the bird that was believed to feed its young with its own blood, symbolizing the continuous reflux of the liquid</note>, the shape of which you see here. In this, one may digest pure things and also circulate each of them. However, these glasses cannot easily be obtained from our glassmakers, as they are laborious to make; but if one orders them, no money should be spared. For as much as they exceed other glasses in price, they bring back just as much profit, being so much nobler and better for all things than any of the others could ever be. Therefore, I advise everyone who requires them for this work to obtain them.

<margin>Difference between fermenting, digesting, and circulating.</margin>

There is also a difference to be understood between **fermenting** or **digesting** and **circulating**. Above all, one must note with all diligence how the glass should be placed. 

Therefore, when you wish to **digest**, take a **cucurbita** with a blind lid. But if the material is purified, put it into a **circulatorium** and make a pit as previously described. In it, make a **stratum** <note>Latin: "layer"</note> of unslaked, finely crushed **lime** <note>original: "Kalck"</note> three or four fingers thick. Upon this, make another **stratum** seven or eight fingers thick of **horse manure** <note>original: "Pferdsmist"</note> that is neither too soft or rotten, nor too hard. And all around the **circulatorium**, lay manure as high as its layer is. Afterward, fill the pit with a foot and a half of earth and put water over the glass, so that it runs down from the manure onto the lime and thus warms the manure. You should change or renew the lime and the manure twice every week. One could also use manure and grape

<margin>Circulation</margin>
If you wish to **circulate** <note>a process where a liquid is evaporated and condensed so it falls back into the original vessel, repeatedly purifying it</note>, you may set it up in the same manner and form as before, except that the uppermost part of the **circulatory vessel** <note>original: "Circulatorio"</note> should not be buried or hidden in the manure. Instead, two parts of the glass should be in the manure or lime, and at most the third part should stand outside in the open air. This is so that the substance which has ascended from the heat may descend again due to the cold. This may also be done in a <term>Bain-marie</term> <note>original: "Balneo Marie"; a water bath used for gentle, uniform heating</note> or in hot sand. Likewise, one may place the material to be circulated in the sun during the summer, or in fresh pomace in the autumn once the grapes have been pressed, as has been mentioned before.

### The Seventh Chapter / How one may extract
### the Fifth Essence without any kind of labor or
### expense.

We have taken note of the saying of Virgil:
"We cannot all do all things" <note>original: "Non omnia possumus omnes"</note>. Likewise, Hesiod says: "God does not give all things to one person alone." And as we see that because of the great labor and expense which this highly esteemed art justly requires, very few wish to walk this path or work in this way—for it is too difficult for some and they become weary of it—we have resolved to show a shorter, easier, and less costly way. For we know well that many lovers of this art might turn away from this most famous craft if they were to read this little book of ours. Therefore, we wish to show those people this other way to extract the <term>Fifth Essence</term> <note>original: "Quintam Essentiam"; the most purified, medicinal spirit of a substance</note> from wine without great labor or cost. Take the very best wine you can get, whether it be red or white, which is a little sweet, and distill it through an <term>alembic</term> <note>original: "Alembicum"; a distilling apparatus consisting of a gourd-like flask and a cap with a spout</note> four times, in the same way we commonly make burned wine or <term>water of life</term> <note>original: "Aquam vite"; brandy or high-proof spirit</note> here; and the more often this is done, the better it will be. Thus, one may recognize through testing that it is sufficiently distilled: when ignited in a silver or tin drinking vessel, it burns away cleanly and leaves no moisture behind; that is a true test. A wine distilled in this way is the most suitable for this work of circulated distillation. Therefore, put this material, thus digested and <term>rectified</term> <note>original: "Rectificierte"; purified by repeated distillation</note>, into the previously mentioned...

# Philippus Ulstadius. VIII

### The Eighth Chapter / On an easy way of
## Circulatory Distillation / and extracting the Fifth
## Essence without Fire.

<margin>Two ways to distill the Fifth Essence without fire.</margin>
If you now desire to avoid all costs and (what is most important) the useless loss of time in this highly renowned work / then you may take up this twofold way of extracting the Fifth Essence for yourself.

The first part of this way is this: Take **horse manure** <note>original: "Roß-mist"; decomposing manure was frequently used by early chemists as a source of steady, gentle heat</note> / put it into a large, wide, and deep vessel / or into a pit made for that purpose / and set the **distillation vessel** <note>original: "Distillatorium"</note> in the middle of the manure / and let a third of it be empty / and stand outside the manure / for nature requires this / so that the substances may rise and fall / and subsequently be converted into a very clear water / And this happens without any labor or fire / but yet one must renew the manure at least once every week. And this may also be done in **grape pomace** <note>original: "Treberen"; the skins and stems left after pressing grapes</note> / as was said above / if one takes them in the autumn during the time of the **vintage** <note>original: "Weinlesen"</note> as soon as they come out of the wine press / Therein you will also see a wonderful effect.

Or it may also be done in the sun during the **Dog Days** <note>original: "Hundstagen"; the hottest weeks of summer, traditionally associated with the rising of the Star Sirius</note> / and for this reason Divine providence has provided for the poor as well as for the rich / so that they too may attain this art. One may also extract the Fifth Essence from cloudy / unclean / and foul wine / provided it is not vinegar / For then it is to be expected / that the Fifth Essence has evaporated and vanished / as **Raymond Lull** <note>original: "Raymūdus. Lul."; a major 13th-century figure whose works heavily influenced later alchemy and medicine</note> says in the third chapter of his first book / And even if a wine is foul and of a very bad substance / nevertheless the Fifth Essence always remains within it / which may be recognized by this / Even if a wine is already spoiled / having grown in a good place / and has in some way become cloudy or tasteless / we see clearly / That a good **Water of Life** <note>original: "Aqua Vite"; high-proof distilled alcohol</note> is still made from it / and therefore such wine is not to be rejected by the poor / because even if the wine is not rightly formed and is spoiled in several ways / yet its Fifth Essence is in no way damaged.

<margin>The other way.</margin>
The other way to extract the Fifth Essence without any labor or fire / is this. Take the most noble **Water of Life** /

...that you may find, put it into a glass with a long neck that has a hole at the very top, which one should close and **seal with lute** <note>original: "Verlutieren"; to seal a vessel airtight using a paste of clay, lime, or wax</note> using such wax as will be taught hereafter, so that it remains airtight. Turn the glass over and place it with the neck into manure, as has often been said before, so that the coarseness of the materials settles at the bottom <note>In this inverted position, the "bottom" refers to the part of the neck that is now lowest</note> of the distillation glass. And when it has been buried in the manure for a long time, then draw it out again carefully, in the same position as you put it in. You may clearly see its form here in the illustration.

Then you will see the separation of the clean from the unclean, and the subtle from the coarse; all coarse matter will be in the neck of the glass. Therefore, pierce through the wax from below with a pointed awl until you reach the water, then pull the awl out. In this way, the coarseness of the earthly matter that settled in the neck of the glass will flow out. And when you have completely divided or separated them from one another, down to the **subtlety of the Fifth Essence** <note>original: "Subtilitet Q. E."</note>, then plug the glass with a finger and turn it over. What remains in the glass is the **Fifth Essence**; however, it is not worth as much or as precious as that of which we spoke above. In the same way, you may bury **burning water** <note>original: "Aqua ardens"; high-proof distilled alcohol or brandy</note>, or distilled wine, upright and not overturned in manure for a time, and thereafter strain off the coarse matter that settled at the bottom from the subtle matter that remains in the neck. But this is also not as powerful as the first; nevertheless, it still draws to itself all the powers of herbs and other material things, and therefore it is not to be entirely despised. There is also found a much easier way for the poor, who cannot always afford the cost of oil and other things, or the diligence required for this work. But I would advise the first way as best, for it is the most excellent and noble, and is achieved with little labor and cost. Do not regret a little money and time, for if you bring it to an end, you will find a thousandfold benefit for human life therein.

<margin>The Fifth Essence from new must.</margin>
But if in the autumn you wish to draw the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: "Quintam Essentiam"</note> from a cask full of new wine or **must** <note>freshly pressed grape juice</note> that is still fermenting, do it thus: If you wish to draw the **spirits** <note>the volatile, active components of the liquid</note> out of the cask without any harm to the wine, have a pipe the size of an arm made from hard, strong, and very dry wood, and likewise a straining tool which is a thumb's thickness inside and at the bottom, or a little thicker, and two hands' breadths long. Press the pipe into the cask so that it cannot be easily removed; seal the cask everywhere as best you can. And when all things are well-sealed and closed, push three or four straw stalks alongside the pipe (otherwise all the wine would run out and go to waste), and upon the pipe set an **alembic** <note>original: "Alembicum"; the cap or "head" of a still used to condense vapors</note>. Wrap it all very well with a linen rag and seal it with the **Lute of Wisdom** <note>original: "Luto sapientie"; a high-quality airtight sealant made of clay, dung, and other binders</note>, and on the other part of the cask place a **receiver** <note>original: "Receptackel"; the vessel used to collect the condensed distillate</note>. And let it stand thus,

# The Ninth Chapter / How one should
## extract the Fifth Essence
### from the Four Elements, in which one 
### may dissolve Gold to create 
### Potable Gold. <note>original: "Auro Potabili"; a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of liquid gold, believed to have universal healing powers</note>

<margin>Experience is the master of all things.</margin>
Enough has been said above regarding what the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: "Quinta Essentia"; the purest, most medicinal part of a substance, beyond the four physical elements</note> is, as well as its effects and the ways and means of drawing it out from its source materials. This has been shown for the poor as well as for the rich, and with it one is accustomed to extracting the powers not only of gold, silver, and all other metals, but also of noble and precious stones and herbs, if they are soaked therein. However, I do not doubt that there are still many diligent students who are not yet satisfied. Therefore (in my opinion) it will be necessary to bring forth things that I have learned through much reading, hearing, and **experience** (which is, after all, the master of all things). Namely, a way in which one may draw the Fifth Essence from wine, in which gold, silver, and other metals—as well as pearls and precious stones—may be dissolved to make **Potable Gold**, or a golden drink. (This, however, is more suited to the **artists** <note>original: "Künstnern"; here meaning alchemists</note> who explore the streams of **alchemy** <note>original: "Alchimey"</note> than it is to physicians). And although there are many ancient **writers** <note>original: "Scribenten"</note> who teach a way to extract the Fifth Essence from wine by the separation of the four elements—so that they may be brought back into one substance—this essence, having been so refined and drawn out from the moisture and all superfluity of the four elements, may be called the Fifth Essence for the dissolution of gold. And we may not only dissolve it herewith, but
