as our elders or ancestors have had and done. And therefore the righteous is snatched away, so that wickedness might not pervert the understanding; the bloodthirsty and deceitful shall shorten their days, and therefore all that we strive for is all vanity, etc.

# The Seventeenth Chapter / How one
## should extract the Fifth Essence from
## Antimony / that is / from
## Stibnite.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'D' featuring foliage and scrollwork motifs.</image-desc>

The <term>Quinta Essentia</term> <note>Fifth Essence; the purest medicinal spirit of a substance</note> extracted from <term>Antimonio</term> <note>original: "Antimonio"; Antimony</note> or <term>Spießglaß</term> <note>original: "Spießglaß"; the historical name for stibnite or antimony ore</note> dries out wounds completely and is made thus: Take the finely pounded powder of antimony, mix it with distilled vinegar—the best you can obtain—and let the antimony remain in it until the vinegar becomes completely red in color. Afterwards, strain the vinegar and put it into a clean vessel, and pour other distilled vinegar onto the powder and set it upon a small fire until the vinegar becomes colored; do this as often as necessary until the vinegar stops taking on color. 

Thereafter, put the colored vinegar into a <term>Cucurbitam</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for boiling</note> and distill it in an ash furnace. First, the vinegar itself will distill over, and then you will see coming out through the beak of the <term>Alembici</term> <note>the "alembic" or still-head</note> a matter of a thousand different colors. And that is the Fifth Essence, which is called by the philosophers <term>Plumbum Philosophorum</term> <note>original: "Plumbum Philosophorum"; literally "Lead of the Philosophers"</note>, which is to say, the Lead of the Wise Masters. Some artists or craftsmen call it "Virgin’s Milk" <note>original: "Jungfrawen Milch"; a term often used for white, cloudy alchemical precipitates</note>. However, it differs from that which will be discussed hereafter, and in color it almost resembles <term>Oleo benedicto</term> <note>original: "Oleo benedicto"; Blessed Oil</note>, or in English, the "Blessed Oil." Therefore, put this into a <term>Pellican</term> <note>a specialized circulatory vessel with two arms that feed condensed liquid back into the base</note> to circulate for forty days; then you may use it in place of balsam for all wounds, for it heals them easily and quickly. It is also exceedingly good for all <term>Apostemen</term> <note>original: "Apostemen"; deep-seated abscesses or swellings</note>, glands, or sores.

One also extracts the Fifth Essence from lead or <term>Bleiweiß</term> <note>white lead; a pigment made of lead carbonate</note> just as from antimony, in this way: Pour distilled vinegar four fingers high upon it, as was said regarding the Fifth Essence of flowers and herbs. Thereafter, distill it, and you will see first the vinegar rising up, and after it a moisture like an oil, and that is called <term>Oleum Plumbi</term> <note>original: "Oleum Plumbi"; Oil of Lead</note>, that is, a Lead Oil, or the Fifth Essence of lead; it has a sweetness within it, like the oil of antimony.

<margin>For burns and mange.</margin>
It is also good for all burns, whether from fire or hot water, and against the <term>Räude</term> <note>mange or scabies</note> and scabbiness, as well as against blisters and warts swollen from heat. However, it should be noted: you should first wash the white lead cleanly and well several times with rose water and strain it through a linen cloth until no more of the powder of the white lead remains in the cloth. Thereafter, you should dry it and keep it until you have need of it.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical distillation of Antimony and Lead using vinegar to produce "oils" or Fifth Essences. These substances are praised for their medicinal properties in healing wounds, burns, and skin conditions like mange.</summary>

Philippus Ulstadius.

And thus it is desired by the <term>Servitor</term> <note>original: "Seruitor"; referring to a traditional pharmacological text attributed to the "Servitor," often associated with the preparation of medicines</note> and also Johannes de Sancto Amando in his commentary on the *Antidote-book* of Johannes Mesue <note>Johannes de Sancto Amando was a 13th-century physician; Johannes Mesue refers to a famous, though semi-mythical, Arabic medical authority whose works were foundational in European pharmacies</note>.

# The Eighteenth Chapter / 
## How one should extract the 
## Fifth Essence from Gold / 
## serving more for alchemy 
## than for medicine.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a tall, narrow, cylindrical ceramic jar with a rounded base, described in the text as an "Irdine gschirr" or "Büchse" that a potter should make for the alchemical process.</image-desc>

Many people have shown various ways and means of obtaining the <term>Quinta Essentia</term> <note>Fifth Essence; the purest, most spiritualized form of a substance</note> of gold. However, for the sake of brevity, we wish to show and teach the most ingenious and skillful way to extract the Fifth Essence from gold. Therefore, if you wish to extract the Fifth Essence from the most precious and perfect metal, take four <term>lots</term> <note>a historical unit of weight, roughly 15–16 grams</note> of gold and twenty-four ounces of <term>living mercury</term> <note>original: "lebendigens Mercurij"; quicksilver or liquid mercury</note> which has been washed and purified in the best possible way, and make an <term>amalgam</term> <note>a mixture of mercury and another metal, in this case, gold</note>. 

And have a potter make you three, four, or more earthenware vessels from the very best clay, in the shape of a canister or box, of such a width and length from one end to the other as is illustrated here. They should be round at the base, a hand-breadth wide, and just wide enough so that a goldsmith’s crucible may fit inside. They should be glazed on the inside and around the rim, hoop, or mouth-hole. When they are made thus, you shall level and smooth the top of the mouth as best as possible. 

Next, take three or four <term>crucibles</term> <note>original: "Crusel oder Tiegel"; heat-resistant melting pots</note> and set them in a fire until they glow. Then take one of them out and set it upon a marble stone, pour the amalgam into it, and quickly, in the blink of an eye, place one of the aforementioned earthenware vessels over it. Let it cool thus upon the stone. 

Afterward, use a <term>hare's foot</term> <note>a traditional tool used by alchemists and goldsmiths as a soft brush to collect fine powders or droplets</note> to cleanly brush down the mercury that has risen from the crucible to the bottom of the upper vessel, and put it back into the crucible with the amalgam. Then put the amalgam into a clean shell; rub and stir it well with a small iron rod or pestle. You will see a subtle and very light black matter rise up like fine dust. Take this off with a feather and put it into a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel</note>, which you must seal as well as possible with <term>Lute of Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Luto Sapientie"; a specialized alchemical paste used to hermetically seal vessels</note>. 

Afterward, take the amalgam and put it into another glowing crucible, and overturn one of the aforementioned vessels over it, letting it cool on the marble stone. Continue to do exactly as you did with the first one.

If you wish to proceed more nimbly, divide the amalgam into three or four parts and put each part into one of the vessels, exactly as described before. And as soon as the amalgam is cooled, you should always have a glowing crucible ready so that you may put another one in.

S
<meta>catchword: and sweep</meta>

<summary>This chapter provides detailed technical instructions for extracting the "Fifth Essence" of gold by creating a mercury amalgam and using specialized earthenware vessels to capture a fine black dust produced by heat. Ulstadius emphasizes the mechanical process of collection using tools like a hare's foot and a feather.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific laboratory instructions for the Eighteenth Chapter, focusing on the processing of the gold-mercury amalgam and the purification (rectification) of the mercury itself to ensure the success of the extraction.</meta>

->Of the Secrets of Nature<-

and always sweep the vessel clean with a **hare’s foot** <note>a traditional tool used by goldsmiths and alchemists to gather fine metal dust or powders without scratching the vessel</note>, as was said before; and always return the **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercuriū"; quicksilver</note> that has risen up on the inside back to the **amalgam** <note>the mixture of mercury and gold</note> until you no longer see any gold mixed with the mercury. The black matter of which we have just spoken is called by the philosophers the **Raven’s Head** <note>original: "Rabenkopff"; in alchemy, this is known as the *Nigredo* or blackening stage, representing the death or putrefaction of the substance before its rebirth</note>, and it is the beginning of the true and genuine **Art** <note>referring to alchemy</note>. And although it is black and dark, it is nevertheless gold, which you may prove in the casting. And when you see that the Mercury no longer gives off black matter, it is a certain sign that it has no more gold within it. Therefore, when you have brought it together in this way, you should wash it clean so that the Mercury may be separated from it. This washing proceeds as follows:

Distill well water once through an **alembic** <note>original: "Alembicum"; a specialized piece of distilling apparatus with a cap and a beak</note>, and put the black matter into a glazed jar or vessel; pour the distilled water onto it and move it back and forth with a clean finger. In this way, you will see the separated Mercury settled at the bottom. Thereafter, strain the water with the matter away from the Mercury into another glazed vessel; pour other distilled water onto it and do as before. Do this as often as necessary until the entire matter is separated from the Mercury. Thereafter, cover the vessel containing the black matter well, so that no filth may fall into it, and set it in a safe place. Let it rest there for a day, so that all the matter settles to the bottom. Afterward, strain the matter through a clean white linen cloth and dry it out by a small fire, or set it in warm sand or in warm ashes. Thereafter, put it into a **cucurbit** <note>original: "Cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped chemical vessel</note> and **lute** it <note>original: "verlutier"; to seal a vessel airtight using a paste or "lute" made of clay or other materials</note> in the best possible way, and keep it until you require it or wish to use it. But your Mercury, with which you wish to amalgamate, you should always prepare beforehand.

<margin>**Rectification of Mercury** <note>original: "Rectifierūg Mercurij"; the process of purifying or refining a substance</note></margin>
Take first as much Mercury as you wish; put it into a stone mortar with salt and vinegar. Mix it together with a wooden pestle until the vinegar becomes black. Thereafter, strain and pour the vinegar away from the Mercury, and pour other fresh vinegar onto it; do this as often as necessary until the vinegar no longer becomes black. Thereafter, let it stand until you see the Mercury at the bottom, and then strain the vinegar away from it again. But set the Mercury with the salt in the glazed vessel by the fire so that it dries out. When this happens, the matter will subsequently be turned into a hard substance. Then crush the same again in a mortar with a wooden pestle, always adding clean well water and rubbing or pounding it. And when you see that the matter is black, strain the water from it and pour fresh water onto it. Do this as often as necessary until the blackness goes away entirely from the Mercury along with the salt; then it is prepared for the gold. After this, squeeze it through a linen cloth three or four times, and finally through a **hide** <note>original: "Fell"; likely chamois leather, which acts as a fine filter for liquid mercury</note>, as the goldsmiths do. In this way, you will have the true rectification of Mercury.

<meta>catchword: But (Doch)</meta>

<summary>This page details the extraction of the 'Raven’s Head' (black gold) from an amalgam and provides a step-by-step procedure for the 'rectification' or purification of mercury using salt, vinegar, and filtration.</summary>
<keywords>Raven's Head, Nigredo, alchemy, mercury purification, gold extraction, laboratory apparatus, distillation, rectification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the chemical treatment of mercury, transitioning from the purification of the "Raven’s Head" to the construction of a specialized furnace for downward distillation.</meta>

Philippus Ulstadius. XXII

But prepare it in another way, namely thus: Take **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Quecksilber*</note> and garlic heads, well and cleanly peeled of their skins; crush them to **kill** <note>original: "tödten"; a term meaning to change the physical state of mercury, usually by mixing it with other substances so it loses its fluidity and "shining" appearance</note> the mercury as well as you can, and squeeze the juice through a cloth. Take as much **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> as you wish and rub it with this juice upon a marble stone until it is "killed" and separated into parts, so that one no longer perceives it at all. Afterward, take other **Mercury**, crush it also with garlic, and do with it as before. When this is done, put it into a glazed earthen vessel or a mortar and crush it well with a wooden pestle, adding salt and vinegar. When the matter turns black, drain it off and add other vinegar; do this as often as necessary until nothing of the black matter remains, and thus it will be prepared in the very best way.

Some others prepare or purify it by another method, which is better than that which is done with **Saltpeter** <note>original: *Salnitri*</note> and **Vitriol** <note>a historical name for various sulfate minerals</note>, and it is this: Take a common prepared salt and **sublimate** <note>original: *Sublimier*; the process of heating a substance so it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</note> the **Mercury** with it. When it becomes as white as snow, throw it into cold water until it **coagulates** <note>original: *Coaguliert*; hardens or thickens into a solid mass</note> together. Afterward, take it out of the water and do as you did at first, by the first method; in this way it is **purged** <note>original: *purgiert*; purified</note> or cleansed in the best possible way, with which you may afterward very easily dissolve gold and silver.

### The Nineteenth Chapter / How one should Distill / *by Descent* / that is / Downward.

I believe there is no one who does not know that there are two ways to distill, namely by ascending <note>rising as vapor</note> and by descending <note>dripping downward</note>. Of these, the first is well known to all, but this one—the way of descent—to few. For which reason it seems fitting and useful to me that I show the way of this distillation to beginners or those yet unpracticed in this **Art** <note>alchemy</note>. For through this distillation one may make many kinds of things, such as **Oil of Juniper** <note>original: *Oleum Juniperi*</note>, **Blessed Oil** <note>original: *Oleum Benedictum*; often a distilled oil made from bricks or herbs, prized for medicinal properties</note>, **Oil** of nutmeg, **Oil** of egg yolks, and many other similar things. Such distillation may take place in a four-cornered furnace made of unbaked bricks, and it should be three cubits wide and long, or a little more, one stone set after another, just as one makes a hearth for cooking. It should be one and a half cubits high from the ground on all sides, except for the front—the foremost side—upon which the iron grates shall lie. Upon these, other thin bricks shall lie, which we sometimes call shingles. In the middle, however, it shall have a hole one cubit wide, which becomes a ring through its length, through which a man’s foot might pass. Upon this hearth, a layer of bricks one cubit high shall be placed everywhere.

<sig>f ii</sig>
<meta>catchword: Afterward</meta>

<summary>This page describes two methods for purifying mercury using garlic or salt sublimation, followed by the introduction and structural requirements for a furnace designed for "distillation by descent."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for chemical distillation, moving from the preparation of mercury to the specific mechanical setup of a furnace for downward distillation (distillatio per descensum).</meta>

Philippus Ulstadius. XXII

### On the Secrets of Nature

<margin><unclear>Distillation...</unclear></margin> Afterward, you should have another furnace ready for distilling, and seal all the distilling glasses you wish to close; this is called the **Seal of Hermes** <note>original: *Sigillum Hermetis*; an airtight seal, traditionally made by melting the glass neck of a flask or using a specialized clay paste</note>, as you will sufficiently learn in the next following chapter. Therefore, if you wish to distill **by descent** <note>original: *per Descensum*; a method where the material is heated from above so that the resulting liquids flow downward into a receiver, often used for heavy oils</note>, take a **cucurbit** <note>original: *Cucurbita*; a pear-shaped vessel used in distillation, named for its resemblance to a gourd</note> made of the best clay you can find. Or, if you cannot easily obtain that, make it of copper or brass, and fill it up to the third part with the material you wish to distill. Close it inside with an iron lid, which should be very thin and have many small holes.

A woodcut illustration of a cucurbit, a pear-shaped distillation flask with a long neck and flared rim, shown in a vertical orientation.

The mouth of the cucurbit should be turned downward, just as you see here in the figure. It should extend three finger-breadths into the hole that is in the foundation or base of the furnace. Also, all things must be very well **luted** <note>original: *verlutiert*; sealed with a "lute" or paste made of clay, dung, and hair to prevent the escape of vapors</note>, so that nothing from the furnace can fall down from the top to the floor. In the lowest part of the furnace, place a **receiver** <note>original: *Receptackel*</note> to catch the material that distills out. In the upper part of the furnace's base, build a **charcoal fire** <note>original: *Kolfewr*</note> on all sides; however, it should be as far from the distilling vessel as possible. At first, the fire should be very small, and afterward, one should increase it gradually, and one may also move it closer and closer to the cucurbit bit by bit.

You should also know that every material distills water first, though some more than others, as may be well seen by one who constantly or frequently works with many kinds of materials in this art. Therefore, place a glass cucurbit under the mouth of the upper one, so that it receives what distills down through the base of the upper part into the bottom of the furnace. And when you see an **oil** <note>original: *Oleum*</note> distilling, you should empty the glass and put it back again. You should always increase the fire and move it closer to the distilling vessel or the cucurbit until you see no more oil distilling. Then you should pull the fire back—the further the better—until it goes out and the upper part of the cucurbit has cooled. Afterward, remove the lower part containing the oil and keep it separately.

The reason why the fire should be very small at first and increased only bit by bit until the **fourth grade** <note>the highest level of heat in early modern chemistry, often a direct or intense fire</note> is twofold. First, so that the moisture from which the oil is to be made is not consumed by sudden and excessive heat. This happens most easily with oils made from **egg yolks** <note>original: *Eyierdottern*</note> and **nutmegs** <note>original: *Muscatnussen*</note>. But with others, such as **juniper oil** <note>original: *Wachholder öl*</note>, you may make a fast fire as large as you please. The second reason is that the burning wood might burn or break the cucurbit, or the oil might catch fire and acquire a foul taste. Therefore, in the beginning, one should manage the fire according to the natural course, considering that nature cannot suffer anything violent, as has been proven before by the opinions of **Avicenna** and **Aristotle**.

C iij
<meta>catchword: in the</meta>

<summary>This page describes the construction and operation of a furnace for downward distillation, emphasizing the gradual application of heat to extract oils without burning them. It provides specific instructions on equipment like the cucurbit and the "Seal of Hermes," while citing authorities like Aristotle and Avicenna on the importance of following natural processes.</summary>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for chemical distillation, specifically refining the mechanical setup of a furnace for downward distillation (distillatio per descensum). It transitions from general furnace construction to the specific methods of sealing vessels.</meta>

Philippus Ulstadius. XXIV

in the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence (Quinta Essentia): the most purified and potent form of a substance, usually the concentrated spirit of wine</term> of wine. But in the **Simple Blessed Oil** <term>Blessed Oil (Oleo Benedicto): often refers to "Oil of Bricks," a medicinal oil produced by distilling bricks soaked in olive oil</term>, you should increase the fire at least at the end of the distillation.

You may also make a furnace in this way: Dig a pit in the earth, according to the requirements of the material to be distilled—long, wide, and deep. In this pit, place a glazed pot, well-washed with water to prevent the distilled oils from soaking into the ceramic. On the mouth of the pot, lay a thin iron plate with many holes, so that the pot extends a cross-finger’s breadth <note>original: *zwerch finger*; a historical unit of measurement roughly the width of a finger</note> above the plate. Place one vessel over the other, mouth to mouth. The material you wish to distill should be in the uppermost pot. Seal the vessel well so the material cannot run out. Then, **lute** <note>original: *verlutier*; to seal a joint with a paste made of clay, dung, or lime to make it airtight</note> everything in the best possible way and build a fire around the upper pot, which should be entirely outside the pit except for the mouth, which should be within the thickness of the pit’s edge. However, this second way is not as good as the first for two reasons: First, you cannot easily separate the water from the oil at the first attempt. Second, you cannot easily know when the material is fully distilled unless you know it through much habit and experience.

A furnace may be made in another way, for distilling **by descent** <note>original: *durch absteigung*; a method where heat is applied from above so the product flows downward into a receiver</note>, as follows: Dig a pit in a raised mound, hill, or mountain, and place a distillation vessel in the bottom in the manner described before. Place another on top of it, the bottom of which should have many holes. There should be a covering over this distillation vessel, and from the lower part to the upper, it should be everywhere well-enclosed and **luted**. The rim of the upper pot should be lower than the mound, so that the mound rises above the pot. Then, build the fire in the manner described above.

You may also place a glass under this pot if the lower part of the furnace (from the bottom pot) is open, so that you may recognize when the material is fully distilled.

## The Twentieth Chapter / How to seal the glasses with the **Seal of Hermes**.

<sig>S iij</sig>

XXIIII

### XXIIII On the Secrets of Nature

A noble water or an **oil** <note>original: *Oleum*</note> requires a particularly strong seal, so that the **spirits** or the moving power <note>original: *Spiritus odder bewegliche Krafft*; refers to the volatile active components of a substance</note> may never evaporate or vanish from it. This is also often required for the matter when one wishes to **circulate** or **ferment** <note>original: *Circulieren oder Fermentieren*; alchemy processes involving continuous refluxing and chemical transformation</note> it, so that its power is not weakened or diminished. This closure is called the **Hermetic Seal** <note>original: *Sigillum Hermetis*; an airtight seal made by melting the glass of the vessel's neck</note>, and it is the noblest and most excellent among all other closures, which is done in this way:

The glass or **distillation vessel** <note>original: *Distillatorium*</note> in which you wish to **digest** or circulate should have a long neck and should be filled up to a third part with the material. You should place it into the small hole of the furnace, as was described in the previous chapter, so that the glass neck passes four **cross-fingers** <note>original: *Zwerchfinger*; a traditional measurement roughly the width of four fingers</note> through the hole of the furnace. If the hole is wider than the neck of the glass, seal it everywhere with **lute** <note>original: *Leimen*; a sealant paste made of clay or lime</note> so that nothing can fall past the glass into the lower part of the furnace. The mouth of the glass should stand free in the furnace and not be smeared with any lute anywhere. Afterward, gradually push the fire closer, increasing it little by little, until you see that the neck of the glass is nearly glowing and becomes red, as if it were about to melt. Then take a **blacksmith’s tongs** <note>original: *schmids Zang*</note> that is glowing hot at the tip and squeeze the neck, wringing and choking it together while it glows. Then slowly remove the tongs, and you will see the glass is entirely whole at that spot, just as it is in other places. Then gradually draw the fire back until the glass cools slowly; for if such a sudden cold were to strike it after the great heat, it would break immediately, and all the effort and expense would be wasted. Therefore, no small care must be taken to cool the glass gradually. By this method, you may keep your glasses as many and as long as you wish.

<margin>To open the glass.</margin>
When you wish to open it, however, wrap the neck of the glass with a **sulfur thread** <note>original: *Schwefelfaden*; a string dipped in sulfur to act as a fuse</note>, wound around six or seven times. Ignite it carefully with a small **wax candle** <note>original: *Wachskertzlin*</note>, and when the thread is entirely burned out, the glass will break off at that spot. Then set the neck aside and put in or take out whatever you wish. <margin>Another opening.</margin> Or, have three or four iron instruments made, two **cubits** <note>original: *Ellenbogen*; literally "elbows," a measure of length</note> long, more or less according to your judgment, each of which has two rings at each end. Take one of these that fits you, make it glowing hot, and force it around the neck of the glass; it will then break or snap off very quickly. In this way, you may break off all large and small glasses, and they break off as evenly as if they had been cut down with a **saw** <note>original: *Segen*</note>.

->Philippus Ulstadius.<-

## The Twenty-First Chapter. / How one
## should distill vinegar and human fountain water
## in which one may dissolve all calcined
## metals.

We have said in the preceding chapters of this book that one may dissolve gold in distilled vinegar, and how by the same means the **Quinta Essentia** <note>the "fifth essence," or the purest, most concentrated medicinal force of a substance</note> may be drawn out from lead and **antimony** <note>original: *Spießglaß*; a metalloid often used in alchemical preparations</note>. Therefore, it is necessary that we describe the method of distillation for the beginner or the yet inexperienced. For there is a great difference between distilling a **Water of Life** <note>original: *Aqua vite*; a high-proof distilled spirit or alcohol</note> and distilling vinegar; in a Water of Life, the best matter distills first, but in vinegar, it comes last. Thus, the water or the **phlegm** <note>original: *Flegma*; the watery, inactive portion of a liquid</note> is the last thing in wine, but the first thing in vinegar. 

And so that the best substance of the vinegar runs into the **receiver** <note>original: *Receptackel*; the vessel used to catch the condensed vapors</note>, take the very best vinegar you can find; put it into a **cucurbit** <note>original: *Cucurbita*; the lower, gourd-shaped part of a still</note>, and place an **alembic** <note>original: *Alembicum*; the "head" or cap of the still that directs vapor to the cooling tube</note> upon it. Distill it through the ashes, or in a **bain-marie** <note>original: *Balneo Marie*; a gentle water bath used for heating</note> with a gentle fire, until no more water comes from it. Afterward, test it often on the tongue to see if it has become strengthened in its sourness with a sharp bite; if so, it is time to take away the receiver and place another in its stead, which should be well sealed with **lute** <note>a specialized airtight sealant paste</note>, and increase the fire a little. 

And when you see small white streaks, almost like little clouds, in the alembic, then distill it further until the **spirits** <note>original: *Spiritus*; the volatile, active chemical essences</note> come out. You will then see this vapor rise in the cucurbit and pass from the alembic into the receiver. But when you see blood-colored drops in the alembic, then put another receiver in place again, and distill it until the entire blood-colored matter has passed out into the receiver. This matter is very foul and smells of burning or "scorched" things; therefore, this same vinegar is not good for dissolving calcined gold to make a **potable gold** <note>original: *Aurum potabile*; a legendary medicinal elixir made of dissolved gold</note>, but it is good for **tinting** or coloring metals. For the stench of the burning will cling to the substance of the gold, whereby the potable gold would be more ruined than improved.

<margin>Distilling Urine.</margin>
But if you wish to distill **urine** <note>original: *Urina*</note>, which is the "fountain water" of man, in which one may dissolve beaten or also **calcined gold** <note>gold reduced to a powder through high heat</note>, the same distilled "fountain" may draw out the color to make the often-mentioned potable gold. 

<margin>For the gout.</margin>
This is also very powerful against **gout** <note>original: *Podagram* or *Zipperlin*; an inflammatory arthritis traditionally associated with rich diets</note>, if one wets the feet with it two or three times every day and lets them dry by themselves. 

<margin>For lung disease.</margin>
It also helps those with **consumption** <note>original: *Ptisicis* or *Lungensiechen*; tuberculosis or chronic wasting lung diseases</note>, and it may be used for many other medicinal needs.

<summary>This page explains the specific distillation techniques for vinegar and human urine, emphasizing that unlike alcohol, vinegar's potent "spirits" are collected at the end of the process. These distillates are used both as alchemical solvents for gold and as topical treatments for ailments like gout and consumption.</summary>

...or performed or helpful medicines, which we have omitted for the sake of brevity, as it is also not our intention to list them here; therefore, you shall distill human urine thus: Take the urine of a human who is of a **sanguine** <note>original: *Sanguineus*; a temperament associated with blood, characterized by a ruddy complexion and optimistic disposition</note> or a healthy **choleric** <note>original: *Cholericus*; a temperament associated with yellow bile, characterized by being ambitious and quick-tempered</note> **complexion** <note>the balance of the four "humors" or bodily fluids that were believed to determine health and personality</note>, and who drinks good wine, and who is not yet over thirty-five years of age. Distill it four times in a **bain-marie** <note>original: *Balneo Marie*; a gentle water bath</note>; afterward, distill it in a **pelican** <note>original: *Pellican*; a specialized alchemical vessel with two side-arms that allow the liquid to circulate and "refine" itself through constant evaporation and condensation</note> for forty days, and keep it until you have need of it. This is a precious medicine for use by the healthy and the sick, and it preserves health.

### The Twenty-Second Chapter / How
### all kinds of honey should be distilled,
### which serves for the
### Drinkable Gold. <note>original: *Auro Potabile*; a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid state, believed to grant longevity</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative initial letter D, intricately designed with a serpentine or dragon-like figure forming the inner curve of the letter.</image-desc>

<margin>Autumn honey.</margin>
<margin>Summer honey.</margin>
<margin>Wild honey.</margin>
The very best honey is found where the greatest abundance of the best flowers is. The Greeks tell of many and varied kinds of honey, among which three are the foremost: namely, **autumn honey**, which is made from the flowers of the **heather** <note>original: *Wesels*; likely referring to *Heide* or meadow-flowers common in the autumn harvest</note>; **summer honey**; and the third kind of honey is **wild honey**, gathered after the first rains of autumn. But if neither the Greek nor any of the aforementioned kinds of honey can be found among us, then take the very best of ours that you can find, which should be reddish and thick, and have nothing watery in it. Put as much of this as you wish into a kettle or an iron pot, and add as much clean spring water to it; boil it over a gentle fire, and skim it constantly until the water is entirely consumed; this should be done nine times. At the very last, you should boil it until it returns to its own honey-like thickness. Afterward, **circulate** <note>to heat a substance in a sealed vessel so that the vapors condense and flow back down, repeatedly purifying the liquid</note> it in a **bain-marie** for forty days, which should be warmed to the first degree of heat. Afterward, put it into a **cucurbit** <note>the gourd-shaped base of a still</note>, which should be narrower and taller than those used for distilling other materials; it should be made of earthenware and glazed on the inside. Place it in a **bain-marie** and distill it through an **alembic** <note>the cap or head of the still that directs vapor to the receiver</note>. If the honey should perhaps attempt to rise upward, you should wrap cold, wet cloths around the outside of the cucurbit, halfway up to the alembic. Thus, first a clear water comes out, which you should keep separately, as it is good for making hair grow long and for coloring it. Afterward, a 
<margin>Surgeons use honey-water for wounds in place of balsam.</margin>
yellow and golden water will come out with an oil, and this you should also keep; for surgeons use this second water in place of **balsam** <note>a fragrant, resinous healing ointment</note>, and it is useful for drying out and healing internal injuries, and for cleansing deep wounds, and also for making the hair beautiful. Thirdly and lastly, you should distill it through the **ashes** <note>an ash-bath, which provides a higher, more even heat than a water bath</note>, and a reddish water will come out that draws slightly toward blackness; and it is useful for cleansing and **incarnating** <note>original: *Incarnieren*; the medical process of promoting the growth of new flesh to heal a wound</note>, that is, making the flesh grow in wounds.

### The Twenty-Third Chapter / Of a Common Potable Gold.

Since we have spoken in the chapters written above about the way one should extract the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest, most concentrated medicinal core of a substance</note> from all things that have a form and shape, so that they may be used for the **Potable Gold**, it seemed necessary to us that we also show something of how the **Potable Gold** should be made. Through this, human life may be kept almost free from decay until the goal set by God. For many work in various ways, and each praises his own method, even if it is not worthy of praise. I find four excellent ways through which, in olden times, gold was brought into a drinkable substance. However, so that I do not overly burden those who are just beginning, I will recount and teach the shortest and most common way, since according to the common proverb among the peasants: *The beaten path does not easily let one go astray.* It is a secret extracted from the **Fifth Essence**, in which the ancient philosophers took great pains, yet they kept everything from us or hid it for a reason. For it has a hidden power which Divine Providence has infused into it, through which the young may recover their beauty and lost strength, and through which all old age is driven away—not by changing the actual age of the person, but it makes an old person look young in appearance if they use it, as will be taught further hereafter. And this is the substance and first **Matter** <note>original: *Matery*</note> in which they labored and worked so hard, so that through some manual way they might preserve the human substance and nature from sudden or untimely decay. For all the human race, as the **Stagirite** <note>original: *Stagirita*; a reference to the Greek philosopher Aristotle, who was born in Stagira</note> philosopher testifies, is eager to be protected from decay. But it is ordained by God that we must die once. Therefore **Seneca** <note>a Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: *Nothing is more certain than death, and nothing more uncertain than the hour of death.* And **Paul** <note>the Apostle</note>: *It is appointed for us all to die once.* Is it not also as he says further: *That we all die in Adam?* Therefore it is impossible for us to find an incorruptible **Matter** that preserves us from decay, since nothing under heaven exists (as **Aristotle** testifies) that is free from decay. Therefore it behooves us to find something that approaches incorruptibility, and I well believe that many have therein—

# Of the Secrets of Nature

<margin>The **Fifth Essence** is not entirely perfect, as many have believed.</margin>
Many have labored greatly in this, hoping they might live forever, and have written that the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quintam Essentia*; the pure, medicinal spirit of a substance</note> is so perfect and indestructible that it lacks nothing. However, this is entirely false. For if it were so perfect that it lacked nothing, it would make us live forever, which is against the will of God, as has often been said. But nevertheless, we may find a way and an art to sustain our life until our appointed goal, so long as we keep God before our eyes and honor Him, as is right and proper, and also maintain a regime that is not harmful to our life.

<margin>A **Potable Gold** of Hugo.</margin>
Therefore, Johannes of Toledo and Hugo, both Cardinals, made a **Potable Gold** <note>original: *Aurum potabile*; gold dissolved into a liquid form for ingestion</note> through this method, which they used at all times in all their food and held as a hidden treasure. It is made as follows.

First, and before all things, you should have a water in which one may dissolve gold, silver, and all precious stones. It is made thus: Take <term>Sal Ammoniac</term> <note>original: *Sal Armoniacum*; ammonium chloride</note> and <term>Saltpeter</term> <note>original: *Salnitri*; potassium nitrate</note>, one pound of each. Crush them to powder in a mortar. Afterward, add to it two *lots* <note>a historical German unit of weight, roughly 15-17 grams</note> of living sulfur and two *lots* of <term>Sublimated Mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurij Sublimati*; mercury chloride, a highly reactive alchemical salt</note>. Rub it and mix it all together. Then take six *lots* of these powdered things and distill it with a gentle fire, which you shall increase at the end, as one does when making <term>Strong Water</term> <note>original: *Aqua fortis*; nitric acid</note> or <term>Parting Water</term> <note>original: *Scheide Wasser*; nitric acid used to separate/part gold from silver</note>, when the water will come out with a great turbulence. See to it that you obtain one pound of this water. Keep this in a glass with a narrow neck, well-sealed, like the one shown here.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a round-bottomed alchemical glass flask with a long, narrow neck.</image-desc>

And when you wish to make **Potable Gold**, take two *lots* or one *quintlin* <note>a small unit of weight, about 4 grams</note> of fine gold, or as much as you desire, for you may make it according to your pleasure. Beat it into very thin sheets, as thin or subtle as is possible for you. And for one part of gold, take three parts of Mercury that has not yet been sublimated. Heat it in a crucible until it begins to smell. Then make an <term>Amalgam</term> <note>original: *Amalgama*; a soft paste made by mixing mercury with another metal</note> like a porridge, and amalgamate it for as long as it takes for them to unite well with one another. When it is well amalgamated,

<margin>Another **Amalgam**.</margin>
pour it into fresh, clean water. One may also make the amalgam with the "fat" <note>likely referring to the oily or sulfurous residues</note> that remains in large cannons after they have been fired often. When the material is soft and fluid, it should be poured into the cannons, shaken back and forth, and amalgamated in the best way possible. You should do this often, namely shaking and striking, until it becomes like a porridge. Then weigh it; take as much raw or "living" sulfur as the Mercury and gold weigh together. Put it back into the dish over the fire and stir or shake it until the sulfur is entirely burned away.

After you have taken it from the fire, crush and mix it together again, and set it upon the coals until it begins to turn red. When that has happened, lift it from the fire...

### Philippus Ulstadius. ###

or almost like the indestructible substance / And that is the <margin>The nature of Gold</margin> **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*; in alchemy, the pure, celestial essence of a substance, beyond the four elements</note>, in which the gold is dissolved, and all its powers may be drawn out. For gold suffers no destructibility from the four qualities <note>Heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note>, except through art <note>Alchemical practice</note>, but not by nature. For who has ever seen gold rot in water? Or who has experienced it being burned by fire? Who has seen it draw rust from the earth? Who, finally, has seen it consumed by the air? But all these things are possible in all other substances; and therefore, when the gold is added and mixed into the **Fifth Essence**, it is called **Potable Gold** <note>original: *Aurum potabile*</note>, that is, a drinkable gold, or a golden potion. Therefore, it is a great art to find a subtle way to dissolve gold and bring it into a drinkable form, so that you do not think you are making a **Water of Life** <note>original: *Aquam vite*; distilled alcohol</note> and instead make a water of death. But this **Potable Gold** should be made with a rightly rectified **Water of Life**, so that the four elements are drawn out of the <margin>A warning.</margin> wine, in which they are hidden. So that you also do not make human blood into a foul, stinking matter; if you lose its powers and it draws all the stench to itself from the **Oil** of human blood. And if it is not made from the earth from which the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*</note> is composed or put together, you would not be able to dissolve the gold. If you thought to make a fiery water, that would be strange to hear and harmful to use. And therefore, you should not care if there is little of this earth, for as <margin>Another warning.</margin> little as there is, it still has great power. Beware also that you do not take another incorrect or foreign earth, as some take lime, some wood ash, some burnt tartar <note>original: *Weinstein*; potassium bitartrate crystals from wine barrels</note>, and some finally vine ash, as we have related above in abundance. And beware of this foreign earth, of which **Geber** <note>original: *Geberus*; referring to the famous 8th-century Arab alchemist Jabir ibn Hayyan</note> speaks: It is an earth and medicine that suffers no other foreign mixture, as we have now often said. For this earth gives the **Water of Life** its power when it is made as we have taught above. And therefore, it will be necessary to find a way to separate the four elements, so that one may make a true **Water of Life**, or better, a **Fifth Essence**, in which one may dissolve the gold in two ways. First, with the **Water of Life**, which then draws its power from the Philosopher's Stone to itself. Second, by **calcining** <note>original: *Calcinier*; reducing a metal to a fine powder through heat</note> the gold or otherwise making it into a powder, as will be taught <margin>Separating the four elements.</margin> hereafter. Therefore, if you wish to separate the four elements from the **Water of Life**, or from the wine from which the **Water of Life** is made, you must take the best wine you can get, and plenty of it, and put it into many **cucurbits** <note>original: *Cucurbiten*; gourd-shaped glass vessels used in distillation</note>, so that a good large portion may go into each **cucurbit**. And when the wine is thus distilled, place it in a spot and also keep the **phlegm** <note>original: *Flegma*; the watery, tasteless distillate that comes over after the spirit</note> separate, and always pour the distilled wine onto its **dregs** <note>original: *Feces*; the solid residue remaining after distillation</note>, so that when the **phlegm** is drawn out from the earth (that is, from its dregs), the earth remains at the bottom.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for preparing "Potable Gold," moving from the theoretical nature of the Fifth Essence to the practical laboratory steps of calcination and distillation. # Of the Secrets of Nature #

## XVIII ##

And therefore, if you require the earth—which is a burnt matter, as was said above—then wash it very well with the **Phlegm** <note>original: *Flegma*; the watery, non-alcoholic part of a distillation</note>, as has been shown, so that nothing of the stinking or burning substance remains there; and through this, the **Water of Life** <note>original: *Aqua vite*; distilled alcohol or brandy</note> is purified.

<margin>Another way.</margin>

image-desc: A woodcut illustration of a brick reverberatory furnace (Referber ofen) with a small arched firebox at the base containing a flame.

There is indeed a more subtle way. When the wine has been drawn off from its dregs <note>original: *secessen*</note>, take the **Phlegm**, along with that which remained in the **Distillation Vessel** <note>original: *Distillatorio*</note> from which you separated the distilled wine, and boil it in a kettle until it becomes as thick as soft honey. Afterward, remove it from the fire and let it cool until the next day; then you will see small white stones settled at the bottom, looking almost like **Saltpeter** <note>original: *Salnitri*; potassium nitrate, used here for its crystalline structure</note>. Afterward, strain off the water and the **Oil** <note>original: *Oleum*</note> that floats on top; also take the **Phlegm** and pour it onto the stones or the previously mentioned earth until it is cleansed of its dregs. Then boil it a second time and let it cool as before; you should do this often until the earth is separated from them. Afterward, you shall dry it in a **Reverberatory Furnace** <note>original: *Referber ofen*; a furnace designed to reflect heat back onto the material</note>, as shown above, in a clean vessel that is open at the top; and in this furnace, **Calcine** <note>original: *Calcinier*; to reduce a substance to powder by high heat</note> it for as long as it takes to turn into a purely white substance, like snow. However, know above all things how to moderate the fire, so that the matter does not melt from excessive heat; for if that were to happen, it would be more suitable for **Alchemy** <note>the transmutation of metals</note> than for **Medicine** <note>original: *Artzney*</note>.

image-desc: A line drawing of an alchemical flask with a round bulbous body and a very long, slender neck.

And when the earth is sufficiently **Calcined**, put it into a glass with a long neck, the shape of which you see here, and pour the distilled wine mentioned above over it—which should be separated from its **Phlegm**—and **Putrefy** <note>original: *Putrificiers*; here meaning to digest or break down using gentle heat</note> it in **Mary’s Bath** <note>original: *Balneo Marie*; a gentle water bath</note> for two or three days. Afterward, distill it through an **Alembic** <note>original: *Alembicum*; the cap or "helmet" of a still</note>; then you will see the earth rise with the water up into the alembic like clear crystals. And in this water, one may dissolve thinly beaten gold; yet it would be better if it were gold leaf, as those can be dissolved more easily and turned into a drinkable substance.

<margin>Potable Gold in another way.</margin>

You may also make **Potable Gold** <note>original: *Aurum potabile*</note> in this way: Separate each of the four elements individually and then draw the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quintam Essentia*</note> out of them, in which you may dissolve the gold and turn it into a **Potable Gold**. However, let it be understood that the gold must be beaten into very thin leaves. And thus, when the **Spirit** <note>original: *Spiritus*; the volatile essence</note>—acting as a soul—is drawn from its **Phlegm**—acting as its body—through the alembic, take the **Dregs** <note>original: *feces*</note> or the earth that remained last in the distillation vessel, along with four times as much of its **Phlegm**, and place them for nine days in **Mary’s Bath** until they are well **Putrefied**. Afterward, distill them through an alembic, and a yellow **Oil** will come forth.

<summary>This page describes the laboratory process for purifying "earth" (residue) through washing and calcination in a reverberatory furnace to create a white crystalline base. This base is then used to create a solvent capable of dissolving gold leaf into a medicinal potion known as Potable Gold.</summary>

# Philippus Ulstadius #

<margin>
Gold water.

Dissolving gold.

The power of Potable Gold.
</margin>

And when nothing more can rise up into the **Alembic** <note>original: *Alembicum*; the cap or lid of a still used to collect vapors</note>, you shall know that three elements still remain in the **Distillation Vessel** <note>original: *Distillatorio*</note>; for you will see the water or **Phlegm** <note>original: *Flegma*; the watery, inactive part of a distillate</note> come out clearly, but the **Air** remains at the bottom like a golden **Oil**. After that, you shall separate the **Fire** from the **Earth**; you shall take four parts Phlegm and one part of the Earth, and you shall **Digest** it <note>heat it slowly to encourage chemical change</note> in **Mary’s Bath** <note>original: *Balneo Marie*; a double boiler or bain-marie</note> with a powerful fire. Then you will see a reddish **Oil**, in which the elements of Fire and Water are contained, and thus you will have divided or separated the four qualities. These you shall finally reduce into the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest, most medicinal part of a substance</note> as follows: **Calcine** <note>burn to a fine white powder</note> the Earth in a glass furnace or the like for three weeks; afterward, pour the Phlegm upon it. Digest it seven times and distill it just as often. In this way, you will have **Water of the Sun** <term>Aquam solis <note>A potent alchemical solvent capable of dissolving gold</note></term>—that is, a water of gold—in which you may dissolve thinly beaten gold leaf. And you should know that the more often you distill the Earth over seven times—which we have also called the **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>Lapidem Philosophorum <note>The legendary substance believed to facilitate transmutation and immortality</note></term>—the nobler, more excellent, more powerful, and mightier it becomes. And if you wish to dissolve gold in this water, take the most subtly beaten leaves of gold and put them into a glass with a long neck. And when you have one part gold, pour three parts of the **Water of Life** <note>original: *Aqua vite*; here referring to the highly distilled spirit</note> upon it, which has been distilled seven times from the aforementioned Earth, and place it in Mary’s Bath until it is dissolved. In this way, one may make **Potable Gold** <term>Aurum potabile <note>Drinkable gold, a famous "universal medicine" in Renaissance alchemy</note></term> to sustain human nature, to drive away all sicknesses, and to revive a person even when a physician has given up on their life; therefore, you should always have it with you. It is also to be noted what power that same "Stone" has, through which the effect of the water is accomplished; for where the spirit wills, there it exercises its influence.

<margin>
When this art is to be revealed and to whom it should be hidden.
</margin>

And therefore, whoever gives or reveals this art to the ignorant is cursed; but whoever shares it with the learned, the wise, and the lovers of wisdom and the arts, he is worthy of the crown of laurel leaves. He is worthy of the crown of the cedar tree because of his speech or teaching. And many have sought this art, but few among them have found it. And therefore, when the four elements are separated, keep each one separate, for each has its own particular effect.

It also expels all **Yellow Bile** <note>original: *Colera*; one of the four humors thought to govern health</note> and drives out all phlegm. It produces a great deal of blood and masculine seed. And therefore, those who wish to use it must often undergo **bloodletting** <note>the medicinal practice of withdrawing blood to balance the humors</note>, for otherwise it would force its way out through the veins. And if a person under twenty years old uses it, they will grow so much that everyone would marvel at it. It is also good for refreshing the eyesight if one puts one drop in the eyes every day for a month. It is also exceedingly useful for lost hearing. Youth.

Medicine of life.

It takes away all sickness and the infirmities of old age; therefore, it is called **Elixir of Life** <note>original: *Elixir vite*</note>, namely, a Medicine of Life. And that is the very greatest secret in medicine, of which no physician, but rather the philosophers have written or spoken. As for why the physicians have kept silent about it, I consider it to have happened for this reason: because it possesses a sharpness which it has drawn to itself from the Earth or the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*; the legendary substance of alchemists used to transform metals and prolong life</note>. And therefore it is not very useful for those [who use it improperly], but nonetheless, it is safer than that which is made through **Strong Water** <note>original: *Aqua fortis*; nitric acid</note>.

# The Twenty-Fifth Chapter
## On Potable Gold, without the Earth or Strong Water.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' at the beginning of the chapter, featuring ornate scrollwork and stylized botanical flourishes in a woodcut style.</image-desc>
The way to make a **Potable Gold** <note>original: *Aurum potabile*; gold in a liquid, drinkable form</note> without the sharpness of this Stone or strong water is this. Take honey and put it drop by drop upon a marble stone; add thinly beaten gold leaves to it, and work them often and well together, as if you intended to make gold ink for writing, so that the gold can no longer be seen or felt. And once you have rubbed two or three ounces of gold in this manner, let it stand for twenty-four hours. You may add a common prepared salt to it, but it is not necessary, except that the material might be more quickly crushed or rubbed. And when the material has settled in the specified time, put it into a beaker or a wide silver bowl and pour lukewarm water upon it—water that has been distilled from honey or otherwise from fresh spring water—so the honey separates from the gold. In the same way, the salt is separated from it, if you had chosen to add it. Afterward, strain off the salt and pour other distilled water upon it, so that it settles as before, and dissolve it just as was said; and if you have mixed salt into it, you should do this more often, as if none had been added, until it entirely dissolves or is separated. Afterward, pour water onto it again in the previous manner and put it into a **Cucurbit** <note>original: *Cucurbita*; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</note>; distill it in **Mary’s Bath** <note>original: *Balneo Marie*; a water bath or bain-marie used for gentle, indirect heating</note> so that only the gold remains in the distilling vessel, and the salt and honey separate from it. But if you added no salt, you should rub it all the longer upon the marble stone. When you have entirely distilled the honey from the gold, put it back upon it and distill it again; do this two or three times. Finally, when you recognize for certain that the honey is entirely separated from the gold (as we have taught above), then add the very best distilled vinegar to it—which should be distilled from the best wine that can be had, or from boiled wine—and distill it four times. When you use the vinegar at the last...

<summary>This page describes the Elixir of Life as a restorative for the elderly and details a gentler method for creating "Potable Gold" using honey and manual grinding on marble rather than corrosive acids.</summary>

Philippus Ulstadius. XXIX
h
<meta>catchword: ge-</meta>

last time you distill, then distill three parts, and the fourth part shall remain at the bottom of the glass or distilling vessel <note>original: *Distillatiorij*</note>; for (as was said above) the distillation of wine and of vinegar are contrary to one another, because in wine, the best matter distills first, but in vinegar, it distills last. Therefore, you should pour this fourth part which remained at the bottom onto the gold; let it rest thus for three days, and afterward distill it together. Then add just as much distilled vinegar to it as before. You must, however, place it in horse manure <note>Pferdsmist; used as a source of gentle, consistent heat in alchemy</note> or in a **water bath** <note>original: *Balneum Mariæ*</note>. For in this vinegar, the gold dissolves in the most subtle way; you should afterward separate it from the gold again in the **water bath**. Afterward, pour the **phlegm** <note>original: *flegma*; the watery, inactive part of a distillate</note> of the distilled vinegar onto it, and distill it away from it again in the **water bath**. And finally, pour the phlegm of the phlegm <note>re-distilled watery essence</note> onto it again, distill it again, and do this as often as necessary until you reach the number seven. After it has been distilled seven times, add **Water of Life** <note>original: *Aquam vitæ*; high-proof distilled alcohol</note> to it, and distill it or separate it off again. At the very last, you should pour **Water of Life** onto it again, ensuring it has no phlegm in it, and 
<margin>Distill the honey, the vinegar, phlegm, and Water of Life.</margin>
separate it from it again in the same way; this shall happen twice. In this way, you may separate the honey, the vinegar, the phlegm, and also the **Water of Life** from the gold in the most subtle manner. Thus it is very well digested, prepared, and ready to be dissolved in the digestion or calculation vessel <note>original: *Calculier geschirr*; likely a vessel used for calcination or precise alchemical measurement</note>, or a furnace in the **Water of Life**, which is then the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest, most potent form of a substance</note>.

But if you wish to make **Potable Gold** <note>original: *Aurum potabile*</note>, take the aforementioned dissolved gold and pour the prescribed **Water of Life** upon it—of which twelve parts have been distilled from one part of the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: *Philosophischen Steins*</note>—and set it to circulate in a **water bath** for forty days or longer. Thus the gold resolves into a glass of water <note>a liquid state</note>. But if one wishes to speak truthfully about these things, it perhaps possesses a sourness or sharpness within it from the distilled vinegar or the **Fifth Essence** which is distilled through or from the **Stone of the Wise** <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*</note>. And through this, one may almost dissolve gold as if through **Strong Water** <note>original: *Aqua fort*; nitric acid</note>, yet it is not as harmful as it would be through **Strong Water**. Although some consider it to be not very useful for healthy people, we see at times that the sick benefit from a thing that is harmful to the healthy, and that happens because of the overwhelming and contaminated toxicity of the nature of the sick person. But when healthy people are to drink or take **Potable Gold** so that it may maintain them in their health, our modern philosophers have taken the gold thus powdered and prepared (as has just been said) without the **Fifth Essence** of the distilled vinegar. They poured the true and rectified **Water of Life** upon it, separated from all its phlegm by the aforementioned Stone three or four times, and distilled it again every time. Thus, at the end of the distillation, some moisture from the gold always remained behind. At the last, however, they took one part of that same moisture and twelve parts of the **Fifth Essence**, and placed it in a **cucurbit** <note>original: *Cucurbita*; a gourd-shaped chemical flask</note>.

It emphasizes the "subtle" nature of this preparation, arguing it is more medicinal and less corrosive than gold dissolved in harsh mineral acids like Aqua fortis.</summary>

# On the Secrets of Nature

...done, a <term>blind alembic</term> <note>original: *Alembicum cecum*; a type of still without an outlet pipe, used for refluxing where vapors condense and fall back into the vessel</note> placed upon it, and circulated in a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: *Balneo Marie*; literally "Baths of Mary," a vessel of hot water used for gentle, even heating</note> for at least forty days. Thus you have the true and justified <term>Potable Gold</term> <note>original: *Aurum potabile*; gold in liquid form intended for consumption as a universal medicine</note>, to preserve the healthy from illness and to deliver the sick from their ailments. Yet, something harmful might arise from such a drink, for reasons indicated by us above in the ninth chapter. If a person's stomach should, over time, become coated on the inside by it—through which gilding <note>the author fears that the gold might literally plate the interior of the stomach</note> it might lose its usual digestion. From this, danger could arise for the stomach, as anyone of moderate understanding might notice, even if they are not a doctor. For this reason, we are moved by a desire to help people more than to harm them.

<margin>The true way to make Potable Gold.</margin>
And although some, and those very experienced in this Art, will become my enemies because I make such high art so manifest and common to everyone, we answer them that it is done because we wish to be useful and helpful to everyone, since **Solomon** says: "Hidden wisdom and hidden treasure are of no use." Therefore, we wish to teach the most truthful way to make a <term>Potable Gold</term> according to our small understanding; though it truly does not befit one to reveal such a great treasure, it is made more for the learned and lovers of the good liberal arts <note>the *artes liberales*, the traditional academic disciplines of a free person</note> than for the coarse and ignorant. And therefore, I wish to ask those into whose hands this little book of ours comes: if I have written certain secrets (which do not belong before the irrational), please interpret them in the best light. For I have considered the aforementioned saying of Solomon and also the teaching of **Cicero**, who says: "We are born not for ourselves alone, but also for our good friends." Therefore, I have wished to let this book go forth for the honor and benefit of my home and fatherland. Although it is made as much for the unlearned as for the learned, yet every envious person should know that it has not happened without cause, as the highest God, Father, and Creator of all creatures, lets His sun shine equally over the evil and the good. For he is cursed who seeks his own benefit more than the common good.

And although it may be despised by the inexperienced, I know nonetheless that many will yet highly praise this book of mine. Therefore, I wish to teach the true <term>Potable Gold</term>, which in my opinion no one among our current philosophers <note>referring to "natural philosophers," the scientists and alchemists of the era</note> can oppose if he perceives the natural matters following hereafter. For I know well that there is one way of speaking among the **alchemists** and another among the **physicians**. But since the certain is to be chosen over the uncertain, and the useful over the harmful, I will side with the physicians. Since **Marsilio Ficino** <note>original: *Marsilius Vicinus*; a famous 15th-century Italian scholar and physician</note> says that none of the ancient philosophers ever followed the path to making <term>Potable Gold</term> that ours follow now. For who doubts the gold that they dissolve in <term>strong water</term> <note>original: *Aqua fortis*; nitric acid</note>? Who doubts the **mercury** with which they the...

He defends his decision to publish these alchemical secrets, arguing that knowledge should benefit the common good rather than being kept hidden by a few experts.</summary>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the safety of Potable Gold, shifting from the potential danger of the gold plating the stomach to the hazards of the corrosive substances used to dissolve it. Ulstadius contrasts harsh chemical preparations with a gentler "Cordial" alternative.</meta>

# Philippus Ulstadius

...to make amalgams <note>original: *Amalgama*; a mixture of mercury and another metal, often used in alchemy to "soften" gold</note> (as they say)—who would say they are not harmful? No one, truly, unless he be blinder than a mole. Although I am not against them being used in <term>Potable Gold</term> <note>original: *Aurum potabile*; gold in liquid form</note> for the sick—such as for <term>lepers</term> <note>original: *Ausseszigen*; those suffering from leprosy</note>, for "poison is to be driven out with poison"—but truly I would not advise using these things, considering that they greatly corrode or gnaw at the body, as any person of average understanding or learning may well judge.

In the same way, one must consider that which is dissolved by distilled vinegar or by the <term>Stone</term> <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>. However, it is not as harmful as that which is dissolved by <term>Aqua fortis</term> <note>original: *Aqua fort*; nitric acid, a powerful corrosive used to dissolve metals</note>; yet it still possesses a mighty sharpness, which the <term>Quintessence</term> <note>original: *quinta Essentia*; the "fifth essence" or purest medicinal spirit</note> received from the Stone. If this reason does not seem sufficient to some, one may further prove it from the words of **Galen** <note>a famous Greek physician in the Roman Empire whose ideas dominated medicine for centuries</note>, where he says: "The natural substance cannot suffer violent movement." **Aristotle** agrees with this, and it is to be understood thus: that the Potable Gold we spoke of above cannot be made otherwise than through the excessive fiery nature of the Quintessence. This fiery nature, if it be <term>sublimated</term> <note>purified by heating until it turns to vapor and then cooling back into a solid or liquid</note> beyond its proper measure, dissolves the gold very swiftly and quickly.

Therefore (in my opinion) this same excessive moisture harms human nature more than it benefits. For that reason, it is not without cause that another, more subtle way is to be found, through which we may bring gold into a drinkable substance. Afterward (if it were so), one might add the previously described Potable Gold to this, if a sickness required it, as far as it is necessary for the patient. 

But one should always add some of the "Cordial" Potable Gold, made from noble stones or pearls and the like. Though it is not called Potable Gold—seeing as no gold goes into it when it is made—yet one may call it so because of its excellent virtue or power and fast action. And one may use this on its own at times, or sometimes mixed with the other, if you want it to enter or penetrate the body quickly, since the other does not have such power. We may also use the "Cordial" at times and mix the other into it, according to what the patient's sickness requires. 

And this is the way to use Potable Gold. But before I come to its composition or assembly, I will prove whether it is possible or not. Therefore, first of all, the philosophers highly praise gold for the reason that, among all metals, it is of a more moderate nature, and it is compared to the **Sun** because of its shine and heat. It is also attributed to **Jupiter** for the sake of its temperance or moderation. And therefore it can wonderfully <term>temper</term> <note>balance or regulate</note> natural warmth or heat with cold, and also protect the bodily fluids <note>original: *feuchtigkeiten*; the "humors" or fluids thought to control health</note> so they are not destroyed or spoiled. Therefore we ascribe and attribute to it the power of the Sun and of Jupiter, which then, as if by their office...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Potable Gold, shifting from the preparation methods to the philosophical and medical justification for its use. It argues that gold's indestructible nature makes it the ideal substance for sustaining the human "vital spirit."</meta>

# On the Secrets of Nature

...and by its nature imparts the spirit of life, or the vital spirit. From this, the philosophers prove that there is no better or more suitable thing to sustain human life for a time and protect it from decay than a most excellent and subtle, and nearly indestructible substance, which penetrates into the internal organs. Therefore, one should take thinly beaten gold, mixed with **cordial spices** <note>medicines intended to stimulate the heart and spirits</note> or seasonings, and make it into a powder or dust. Thus says **Johannes de Rupescissa** <note>a 14th-century Franciscan alchemist known for his works on the "quintessence" or fifth essence</note>, that it is necessary for us to take care to perform possible things and flee from the impossible. Since we see many wondrous effects from **lead**, **iron**, **tin**, and **silver**, should we not value or estimate that there is even more or greater virtue or power in one far more precious, namely in **gold**?

<margin>Quenched water binds.</margin>
We also see (as **Avicenna** <note>a famous Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries</note> testifies) that a water in which glowing **steel** is quenched stops the "flow of the belly" <note>original: *den fluß des Bauchs*; a reference to treating diarrhea or dysentery</note> and binds the intestines together or fastens them to one another, which may also be seen with **iron**. How much more, then, is this to be trusted to gold, in which there is nothing destructible of the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—the fundamental building blocks of matter in ancient science</note>, as there is in iron, steel, and the others? For if steel has the power to force the intestines together and knit them to one another, might this not be even more possible for gold, since it is more finely purified and more indestructible than steel?

<margin>Hardening lead.</margin>
Secondly, one may also prove this through obvious reasons and experience: when molten **lead** is frequently poured into a water in which **gold**, **steel**, or **iron** have been quenched, we see that it becomes hard. From this we can clearly recognize or infer that the same water has drawn out the power from the material that was quenched in it. Conversely, we see daily that if molten lead is poured into water, and afterwards glowing iron, steel, or gold is quenched in it, they become soft from the water, which has drawn that same power out of the lead—a softness they do not naturally possess. 

Therefore, just as we have recognized this through daily experience in iron and lead, so also it is to be noted and understood in gold. And this is the true way among the natural masters of philosophy concerning <term>Potable Gold</term> <note>original: *Auro potabile*; a medicinal liquid believed to contain the essence of gold</note>, as **Marsilio Ficino** <note>original: *Marsilius Vicinus*; a famous 15th-century Italian scholar and physician</note> says. Yet one does not drink its physical substance, but rather its **virtue and power**, drawn out of it through the most subtle ways and means. For it would be false if one thought that it entered into the composition or structure of a human body, as the aforementioned Marsilio Ficino very bravely proves. Or that it is likened to the human body, or turned into a drinkable substance; for one would first have to turn it into blood and then into flesh, which is impossible because of its stability.

Therefore it is to be known that in many things the power of gold [the...]

...strengthens and sustains the human body, though this material substance does not enter into the final composition. Just as the magnet or the diamond—through its brilliance, by which it enters into the gold—draws the same to itself, by which the human body is wonderfully strengthened. Therefore, those who convert gold into a drinkable substance through *aqua fortis* <term>nitric acid; literally "strong water"</term> or (as we said above) through distilled vinegar, destroy or ruin its power more than they improve or ennoble it (if they intend to extract that same power).

### The Twenty-Sixth Chapter: How
### one should make a Potable Gold
### with spices or
### seasonings.

To make a Potable Gold <note>original: *Aurum potabile*; a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid state</note> with certain spices, from which some extract superfluous moisture and others strengthen the principal organs. Therefore, one must use such Potable Gold according to a regimen or diet, regarding sleeping, waking, eating, drinking, filling, and emptying; and it is done thus: Take thirty-two pounds of the very best wine you can get, place it in a *Balneum Mariae* <note>a water bath or double boiler, named after Mary the Jewess, an ancient alchemist</note> and distill it until only one measure remains. Afterward, take other wine and distill it likewise. Do this until you have thirty-two pounds of such distilled wine. Then take all the distilled wine, put it back into the kettle, and distill it until only half remains. Put that into a *cucurbit* <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</note> and distill it until all the watery substance has left. After that, distill it four times in a *Balneum Mariae*.

After this distillation, add these spices: raw *Ligni aloes* <note>Latin: "wood of the aloe"</note>, in German called Paradise wood, in its raw state; *Cardamomi* <note>Latin: "cardamom"</note>, in German, small cardamoms, likewise fresh; fresh cubebs—one *quintlin* <note>a historical unit of weight, roughly 3.7 to 4 grams</note> of each; cinnamon sticks, nutmeg, mace, white ginger, long pepper, grains of paradise, and both types of sandalwood—four *quintlin* of each. Grind all these things into the finest dust, put them in a glass, and pour the distilled wine over them. Seal it well and place it in a *Balneum Mariae* for eight days. Place an *alembic* <note>the cap or "beak" of a distilling apparatus</note> upon it and distill it with a gentle fire. Afterward, put it into a long-necked *cucurbit* and add two *quintlin* of finely beaten gold leaf, along with *Gariofilate* <note>Latin: "cloves"</note>, more cloves, and good ambergris—one *quintlin* of each. You shall also grind these things to a dust and, in a *cucurbit*, well...

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for finishing the spiced "Potable Gold" recipe introduced on the previous page.
