# Regimen of Life

Mixing this water <note>Referring to the distilled water described on the previous page.</note> with wine brings a good memory <note>original: "Memori."</note>; give two drops to the elderly with every meal, and to the young of twenty or twenty-five years, three times a week.

The worthy Master Isaac <note>Isaac Judaeus, a renowned Jewish physician and philosopher whose works were influential in medieval Europe.</note>—as one reads of him—used this water while fasting and also during meals, drinking three or four drops in a spoonful of wine. And he says: any person who uses this water in such a way is protected from all illnesses until their appointed age. You may also use it to rub on your face; it preserves it and makes it beautiful.

It also sharpens the reason and drives away sadness when drunk or smelled. It is also a strong aid to the natural warmth of the bodily spirit when it is converted into blood. On whatever day a person drinks this water or uses it by smelling it, stroke <note>original: "Schlag," referring to an apoplectic fit.</note> or paralysis will not afflict them on that day.

It is good for poisonous and stinking mists <note>References the "miasma" theory of disease, where bad air was thought to cause illness.</note> when they occur; one should rub the nostrils and the temples with it, and smell it through a small sponge; it protects you from all evil airs.

### Another precious and good Water of Life, whose virtue
### cannot be sufficiently praised or
### written of, for all ailments
### and illnesses
### of the body.

Also, take twelve *lot* <note>A unit of weight, roughly 15-18 grams.</note> of noble sage, nutmeg, ginger, cloves, grains of paradise, cinnamon bark—one *lot* of each; rue leaves, laurel oil, castoreum <note>original: "Bibergeil," a secretion from beaver sacs used in traditional medicine for nervous system disorders.</note>—one and a half *quintlin* <note>Approximately 3.6 grams.</note> of each; spikenard, marjoram—one *quintlin* of each; rosemary flowers, zedoary bark <note>original: "Citernarrinden," likely referring to the root of Curcuma zedoaria, similar to ginger.</note>—three *quintlin* of each. All these things are put together, and two *maß* <note>A measure of volume, roughly 1 to 1.5 liters depending on the region.</note> of distilled wine <note>original: "gebrannten Wein," or brandy/aqua vitae.</note> are added. Set it to ferment <note>original: "Putrificieren."</note> for fourteen days, and stir it once every day; afterward, distill it in an alembic, and hang ambergris and musk <note>original: "Ambra, Bisem."</note>—each the weight of a barleycorn—inside it <note>This "hanging" method allowed the rising vapors to absorb the scent and properties of the precious aromatics.</note>.

Also, taking one or two drops of this water during meals or at night protects you from headaches and the graying of hair, especially if you also rub yourself with it. If someone has pain in the mouth, on the cheeks <note>original: "Büllern."</note>, or in the throat, let them hold a little of the water on it; it helps. It is also good for dizziness and fainting <note>original: "geschwinden und Ohnmächtigkeit."</note> if it is mixed with tamarisk water and used. It also prevents the trembling of the limbs.

Also, whoever rubs their face with it makes and keeps it smooth and free of wrinkles. It also restores a person's speech. And it is good for cramping in the belly that comes from superfluous moisture, and it comforts <note>In a medical sense, "to comfort" meant to strengthen or fortify an organ.</note> greatly. It also prevents dropsy <note>original: "Wassersucht," an old term for swelling or edema.</note> that comes from coldness, if one drinks it and rubs oneself with it. It is also good for the impurity of the spleen <note>original: "Milzey."</note> if one part of this water is mixed with two parts of fumitory water <note>original: "Taubentropff wasser," referring to the plant Fumaria officinalis, used for skin and liver/spleen ailments.</note> and...

At this time, this term was used broadly for various chronic skin conditions, not just Hansen's disease.</note> is already completely decayed.

<margin>Cough.</margin>
Also, take two *lot* <note>A historical unit of weight, roughly 15-18 grams.</note> of figs; cut them small. Take raisins, scraped licorice, two *lot* of sugar loaf <note>original: "Panis zuccari," refined sugar sold in a hard, conical shape.</note>, and a small glass full of honey. Boil this in a new pot until it is well reduced; then let it cool and drink it in the morning and evening as warm as you can bear; it helps against the cough.

<margin>Cough, from the head, stomach pain, stinking mouth.</margin>

From these, make a powder and use it morning and evening; it is good for the cough, headache, and the eyes; it also promotes expectoration <note>original: "machet ausswerffen," referring to coughing up phlegm.</note> and strengthens the stomach, prevents vapors from rising into the head, and drives away a stinking mouth <note>Bad breath.</note>.

## A Golden Water, proven for the head and all internal organs, and for the Plague.

Also, take red, white, and yellow sandalwood; red and white coral; tormentil; dittany; white and red bolus <note>original: "Poluß." This refers to medicinal clays, such as Armenian Bole, used as astringents.</note>; dragonwort; adderwort; pimpernel; and *Terra Sigillata* <note>original: "Terre sigillate," literally "sealed earth," a medicinal clay from Lemnos stamped with an official seal to guarantee its purity.</note>, one *quintlein* <note>A small weight, about 3.5 to 4 grams.</note> of each. Add half a *quintlein* of cloves <note>original: "Kronäuglin," literally "little crown-eyes," a common name for cloves due to their shape.</note> and two *lot* of Theriac <note>original: "Tryacker." A famous "universal" antidote and cure-all containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh.</note>. Crush and mix all of this together with four nut kernels, and place them in a half-measure of distilled wine <note>Brandy or Aqua Vitae.</note> and one and a half measures of vinegar for eight days in a tin jug. Afterward, distill it through a glass helm <note>The upper part of a still.</note> and use it every day during times of sickness <note>original: "Siechtagen," likely referring to outbreaks of the plague.</note> as described above, taking about half an eggshell full.

Also, there are seven herbs, more excellent than others, healing and good. By name: the first herb is called pimpernel; 2. Rosemary; 3. Angelica; 4. Gentian; 5. Motherwort <note>original: "Mutterkraut," which could also refer to feverfew in this context.</note>; 6. Lavender; 7. Distilled wine. Therefore, if you extract the juice from these, and even if you were wounded <margin>The wounds.</margin> all the way into the skull—provided you drip such juice into it as a wound should be healed—it heals quickly. Likewise with other wounds and sores: drip it in and gently soften the injuries with a damp cloth, then bind a simple plaster or a leaf of one of the same herbs upon it; it heals very quickly. And to prove it is true: pierce the skull of a rooster—though not all the way through—and drip the aforementioned juice of pimpernel and the others as stated above into the rooster’s skull once or twice; it will visibly heal back together.

Also, for those whose eyes are dark <note>Meaning dimmed or clouded vision, such as cataracts.</note>, take white myrrh and crush it into a fine powder. Mix this with honey that has been well-boiled over a glow, and then salve the eyes with it; you will immediately sense the clarity and soothing of the eyes.

<margin>Dark eyes.</margin>
For dark eyes, take the juice of celandine <note>original: "Schellkraut." Traditionally called "eye-wort" because swallows were legendary for using it to heal their young's eyes.</note>, and when you go to sleep, put it in the eyes; it makes them exceedingly clear.

LVI
### Regiment of Life

<margin>A good eye powder.</margin> Also, take one *lot* <note>A historical unit of weight, approximately 15–18 grams.</note> of **gentian** and scrape the outside off; take an iron spoon and quench <note>original: "lesch... ab." A process of heating a substance and then dipping it into liquid to temper or infuse it.</note> the gentian into **Malvasia wine** <note>original: "Malvasier," a sweet, highly prized Mediterranean wine.</note> nine times. Afterward, grind the quenched gentian finely in a mortar; also take ten pieces of **fresh ginger**, each as large as a bean, and also take a little **powdered pearl** from the apothecary. Grind it all together and sift it through a green silk cloth. Whether you are traveling across the land or at home, sift a little of the powder from the cloth onto a paper when you wish to go to sleep, and then put a little of this powder into the eyes; it is better than all other things and makes the vision clear.

Also, for those whose eyes run or drip: take dry **vine leaves**, **dwarf elder** <note>original: "Attichkraut," *Sambucus ebulus*, often used in traditional medicine for its anti-inflammatory properties.</note>, and **honey**, all mixed well together and pounded. Squeeze the juice through a cloth and smear it on the eyes; it makes them beautiful and dry.

<margin>Nosebleeds.</margin> Also, when the nose bleeds: take **burnt clay** <note>original: "Leymen," loam or clay.</note> from an oven, or wherever you may get it, put it in strong vinegar, and smear the paste into the nose with a finger; it will stop soon. Or take **eggshells** and put them in vinegar until they become soft; then rub them into a paste and smear that into the nose; it helps.

Also, take **nettle root** and **rue root** <note>Rue (*Ruta graveolens*) was a staple of the medieval herb garden, often used to treat headaches and circulatory issues.</note>, washed; hang them around the neck; it stops the nosebleeding.

<margin>For the sniffles.</margin> Also, take **theriac** <note>original: "Tyriac," a complex herbal "wonder-drug" used as a universal antidote since antiquity.</note> and **sage**; boil them together in wine and sniff it often while it is warm; the cold will pass, and it is healthy for the sniffles.

<margin>For the "Orazel."</margin> If an *Orazel*—that is, a small worm or an **earwig**—should get into someone's ear, which can cause a person to waste away, take **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "Sal armoniaci," ammonium chloride, used in alchemy and medicine.</note> and rub it in pure water until it dissolves. Then take the water and let a drop fall into the ear; the *Orazel* will die from it. Afterward, it will come out, or one may pull it out with the head of a needle.

<margin>To make one hear well.</margin> Take **breast milk**—the best would be from a woman who has delivered a child within two weeks—and take **hartwort roots** <note>original: "Hirschen wurzlen." Likely *Seseli* or another botanical associated with deer in folklore.</note>; wash them clean, pound them, and squeeze the juice through a cloth with the milk. Drop three or four drops into the ears for one to four days; then the hard-of-hearing shall hear well.

<margin>For swellings behind the ears.</margin> Also, take **nettle leaves** and **salt**; pound them in a stone mortar and make a plaster of them. Lay or bind it over the swelling behind the ears, and it will go away, whether for young or old.

<margin>For one who does not hear well.</margin> Also, a good art for one who does not hear well: take one or two **onions** and hollow them out. Pour **olive oil** <note>original: "Baumöl," literally "tree oil," usually referring to olive oil in this period.</note> into them, along with **wormwood juice**, juice of **shallots** <note>original: "Eschlauch," referring to scallions or shallots.</note>, and **breast milk**, all poured together in equal weight. Put this back into the onion as before, then place it in **hot ashes** until the material ferments together and the onion appears as if it were burnt. Then crush the onion with the mixture, put it in a small cloth, and squeeze the juice through. Afterward, one should put a warm drop of the oil into the ears and then plug that same ear with **cotton**.

<summary>This page provides a series of practical folk-remedies for treating ailments of the eyes, nose, and ears, featuring a mix of botanical ingredients, animal products like breast milk, and minerals like sal ammoniac. It highlights the period's belief in "ear-worms" as a cause of illness and the importance of warm infusions for treating respiratory and sensory issues.</summary>

<keywords>gentian, theriac, earwig, hearing loss, eye health, nosebleeds, breast milk, traditional medicine, medieval pharmacy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical advice attributed to Marsilio Ficino, moving from the care of the eyes and nose (on the previous page) to the treatment of the ears, mouth, teeth, and complexion. The remedies continue to rely on a mixture of herbalism, common household staples like rye bread and vinegar, and more exotic imported spices like galangal and cloves.</meta>

LVII
### Marsilio Ficino. LVII

...plug [the ear] and lie on your side for three or four hours; afterward, the person should turn over and clean the ear, for he will find much filth <note>original: "wust," referring here to earwax or discharge.</note> within; this is a proven and true art.

<margin>For a stinking mouth.</margin>
Also for a stinking mouth: take **rye bread crusts** and **galangal**, powder them with a few **cloves**; it helps well.

<margin>Stinking breath.</margin>
When a person’s mouth tastes bad from a fever or from an illness: take cold **spring water**, salt it well, and eat as much warm **rye bread** with it as you like. Do this for three days while fasting <note>original: "nüchtern," meaning on an empty stomach, usually in the morning.</note>, and the mouth will taste good from the inside out and it will be pleasant to eat.

<margin>Stinking mouth.</margin>
When someone’s mouth tastes or stinks, a clever art: take **myrrh** under the tongue and eat **sage**; it helps well for the taste and the stench in the mouth.

Also, **cloves** or **Paradise wood** <note>original: "Paradiß holtz," also known as Lignum aloes or agarwood, a highly prized aromatic resinous wood.</note> chewed in the mouth is good for a foul-tasting mouth.

Also, **Sweet Musk Powder** <note>original: "Dyamusci dulcis," a cordial confection made of musk and various spices used to strengthen the heart and sweeten the breath.</note> or Gabriel’s **Aromatic of Roses** <note>original: "Aromaticum rosarum Gabrielis," a medicinal rose-based aromatic compound.</note> is good for the taste of the mouth and the teeth. But if the taste is from rot in the mouth, then wash it with **sage wine** and keep **myrrh** in the mouth for a while; afterward, wash it again with sage wine and take a little piece of **galangal** in the mouth.

<margin>Sores in the mouth.</margin>
Whoever has sores or little blisters in the mouth: take the juice of **pointed plantain** <note>original: "spitzen Wegrich," *Plantago lanceolata*, commonly used in the Middle Ages for its healing and astringent properties.</note> in the mouth; always take it fresh and hold it therein, or chew the leaves; this heals them very easily.

<margin>For speech.</margin>
Also, for one whose speech is impaired so that he cannot speak well: take the juice of **vine leaves**, put it in **vinegar**, and drink that; the speech will return. Or boil **pennyroyal** <note>original: "Boley," *Mentha pulegium*, an aromatic herb in the mint family.</note> in good wine or vinegar, and afterward, one should put that water on the tongue of the speechless person; he will immediately speak well because of it.

<margin>Toothache.</margin>
For one whose teeth ache: take good **cloves of ginger**, cut them small, and boil them in **wine**. Wash the teeth with this while it is warm and while fasting; it helps.

<margin>Teeth.</margin>
Take **pepper**, **frankincense**, and **dried sage**; make it into a powder and then take the **white of an egg**. Make a little salve from this and lay it on the cheeks or the jaw once or twice; that resists and drives away the "shots" <note>original: "schüß," likely referring to sudden, sharp shooting pains in the teeth.</note> of the teeth.

<margin>Tooth pain.</margin>
Also, for one whose teeth ache so that the jawbones are swollen: he should let **pellitory** <note>original: "Bertramer," *Anacyclus pyrethrum*, a root known for its pungent heat that stimulates saliva and was a standard remedy for toothaches.</note> boil in **vinegar**; take the boiled liquid into the mouth while it is hot; then spit it out and take more. Do this four times; it drives away the tooth-sores and all pains of the teeth.

<margin>Bleeding of the gums.</margin>
Also, for one who collects much blood in the gums, which will usually begin to smell: take **plantain juice** and wash the teeth with it; that drives away the excess blood and the taste or smell.

<margin>For yellowing of the face.</margin>
Also, for one who has spots <note>original: "schneblizen oder Angel," referring to facial blemishes, freckles, or yellowish discolorations.</note> on the face: take **linseed stalks** and dry them; pound them into a powder and then take pure **honey**. Mix them together and smear the face with it; let it dry on its own. Do this once or three times, and always wash it off with a **young boy’s urine**; the spots will pass and it will make a clear, soft face.

<summary>This page provides practical remedies for oral hygiene, toothaches, and speech loss using botanical ingredients like plantain, galangal, and pellitory. It also includes a cosmetic treatment for facial blemishes involving linseed, honey, and the traditional use of "clean" urine.</summary>
<keywords>oral hygiene, toothache, plantain, galangal, myrrh, pennyroyal, pellitory, skincare, Marsilio Ficino, folk medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical advice from the "Regime of Life" (Regiment des Lebens), likely following the previous page's focus on the head and face. It transitions from oral hygiene into treatments for the skin, throat, respiratory system, and specific concerns regarding women's health and nursing.</meta>

# Regime of Life

<margin>Red spots on the face.</margin>
Item, if you wish to drive away the red marks that one has on the face, take **fennel** and pound it small; then take old **Reinberg fat** <note>original: "alt reinberger Schmer," likely a specific type of animal fat or lard from the Reinberg region.</note> and pound it together a little more, and smear your face very gently with it; it will pass, and you will become well-formed.

<margin>To make a beautiful face.</margin>
Item, take **beans** and place many of them in strong, good **vinegar**; let them lie therein for a day and a night until the skin comes off; afterward dry them in the sun, then grind or powder them; then add beautiful warm water to it so that you may wash yourself with it; and when you wish to go to sleep, wash the face with it, then it will be beautiful and lovely in the morning.

<margin>Sores in the throat.</margin>
Item, give warm water in the throat to gargle that is made from **myrrh**; it is very well-proven. Or take **St. John’s wort**, **rue**, **sage**, and **self-heal** <note>original: "Brunellen," *Prunella vulgaris*, a common herb used historically for throat ailments and "the king's evil."</note>, a handful of each; boil it in two measures of water until half remains; then strain it and mix in half a pound of **rose honey** and seven **lots** <note>A "lot" was a traditional unit of weight, roughly 15-16 grams.</note> of **mulberry juice**; with this, let the patient <note>original: "Siech," meaning the sick person.</note> wash the throat; it helps, and many have been helped by it.

<margin>Heavy breathing.
Cough.
Goiters.</margin>
Take **nettle roots** and boil them in good old **wine**; eat and drink from it; that is good if someone has a heavy, short breath, and it drives away the cough. Item, if a **goiter** <note>original: "Kropff," a swelling of the neck resulting from enlargement of the thyroid gland.</note> wants to grow on someone, let them take **celandine** <note>original: "Raußwurz," likely *Chelidonium majus*, used in traditional medicine for various growths.</note>, **sheep's tallow**, and **salt**, pounded together in equal parts and then placed upon the goiter; that makes it vanish and drives it away.

<margin>Woman’s milk.</margin>
If a woman has no milk to nurse her children, let her take green **fennel**, put it in **wine**, and drink it while fasting for one day or five; she will gain natural milk.

<margin>A woman's time.</margin>
Also, if a woman does not have her time <note>A reference to amenorrhea, or the absence of menstruation.</note>, one should take **sage** and **rue** with the herbs on top, and also take **southernwood** <note>original: "Abrotanum," an aromatic herb in the wormwood family used to stimulate menstruation.</note> and **soapwort** <note>original: "Seyffen," likely referring to the herb *Saponaria officinalis*.</note>, as much as you think is enough; put it in a pot and add water; set it on the fire so that the pot begins to boil; then cover it well and take the pot from the fire; set it in a place where you may sit over it <note>This describes a "sitting-bath" or vaginal steam treatment common in early modern medicine.</note>, and cover the pot so that you are otherwise well-covered around yourself, so that the steam may go into you from the pot through a pipe; this helps beyond all skill.

<margin>Too much of a woman's time.</margin>
Item, take **hartshorn** <note>original: "Hirzhorn," the antlers of a male deer, often rasped into powder for its supposed astringent properties.</note> and shave from it a very fine powder; put that into fresh spring water and let the woman drink from it, so her sickness <note>original: "Siechtag," here referring to the menstrual flow or the period of illness.</note> will stop; you should also not take too much of the horn, so that it does not stop [the flow] too much.

Item, whichever woman has too much of her time, let her pound **nightshade** until it becomes moist and soft, and lay it [on the body] as she goes to sleep, etc.; it helps and diminishes it for her.

<margin>Itching scabs.</margin>
Whoever has broken out on his body such that one thinks he is like a leper, let him take many **asphodel roots** <note>original: "Goldtwurtzlen," literally "Gold-roots," also identified in the text as *Affodillus*.</note>; boil them in pure water, and then make a **sweat bath** in a tub, well-covered; and have within it hot, glowing pebble stones or smithy stones from a smith; then pour the aforementioned water that you boiled with the asphodel, called **Affodillus**, onto the hot stones so that it steams well once or thrice; and put the head out of the tub so that you do not become faint; then the deformity passes, and a beautiful, well-formed skin comes from it.

Item

### Marsilio Ficino

<margin>Bedbugs.</margin>
Also for **bedbugs** <note>original: "Wandtleuß," literally wall-lice.</note>, take the herb with the seeds from the tops of **hemp stalks**, and lay it by your bed or in your bedframe; they will not stay there. Or, smear the cracks of the bedframe with **spirits** <note>original: "branntem Wein," meaning distilled wine or brandy.</note>; they will die.

<margin>Warts.</margin>
Also, if someone has **warts** on the body, let him take a **straw**, burn the wart well on that limb, and afterward pound **wild cress** and **mustard** together and lay it over the wart; thus they will disappear.

<margin>Tightness of the chest.</margin>
Item, one should take **lady’s mantle** <note>original: "Sinnaw," *Alchemilla vulgaris*.</note>, **cinnamon bark**, and moisten them in a vessel with good **wine** so that it becomes soft; then take **pepper** and as many **vine leaves** as there is of all the rest; put all this into a clean pan and roast it over a glow while stirring well; afterward, one should grind and pound it, and then add good, clear **honey-water** over it and mix it well, like an **electuary** <note>original: "Latwerg," a medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note>; and then eat of it often; it opens up the chest for you and is healthy.

<margin>Stomach ache.</margin>
Item, for one whose belly gripes and hurts, he should take the **plantain** herb and squeeze or pound the juice out of it; make it warm and lay it warm over the belly, keeping it there for a while; it helps well and he will become healthy.

<margin>Bloating.</margin>
Item, take **nettle root** and seeds; pound them, ensuring the root has been washed clean and sliced beforehand; mix them with **honey** and eat of it; it warms the cold lungs and drives away **bloating** <note>original: "Bauchblöen," referring to flatulence or abdominal wind.</note>.

<margin>The Stomach.</margin>
Item, he who has a **weak stomach** <note>original: "vndewigen Magen," likely referring to indigestion or a "slow" stomach.</note> should eat **pennyroyal** <note>original: "Boley," *Mentha pulegium*.</note> in a meal, or pennyroyal seeds; it helps well and is good.

Item, one should take **wormwood** and **rue** in equal measure, and **pepper**, and pound it all together and boil it in **wine**; drink of it; it helps the stomach very well and cleanses it.

Item, whoever has a **cold stomach** <note>In Galenic medicine, a "cold" stomach was thought to cause poor digestion and vomiting.</note>, such that food will not stay within him, should take one part **fennel seeds** and two parts **May honey**, and should cook these together gently over a glow until it is prepared correctly; of this, one should eat two spoonfuls in the morning on an empty stomach; that warms the stomach and is healthy.

<margin>Tertian fever.</margin>
Item, one should take **ribwort plantain** <note>original: "Spigwegrich," *Plantago lanceolata*.</note> roots; dry them and pound them into a powder, and then drink the powder with warm **wine** or warm water on an empty stomach before the shivering <note>original: "ehe denn es jhn rürt," referring to the onset of the fever's paroxysm or "the shakes."</note> affects him; it will never return if this is done a second time.

<margin>Quartan fever.</margin>
Item, take **ribwort plantain** and pound the juice from it, pressed through a clean small cloth, and drink that with **honey-water** <note>original: "Mulsa," a mixture of honey and water or wine.</note> before the fever affects him; it helps very well.

<margin>Difficulty urinating.</margin>
Whoever cannot **urinate** well should take **ribwort plantain** leaves or roots, pound the juice from them, and drink it with good **wine**; that makes the water flow very well and makes the **bladder** quiet and easy.

<margin>Incontinence.</margin>
He who cannot **hold his urine**, and it escapes him against his will, should take **lettuce** <note>original: "Lactuca oder Lattich."</note> leaves that grow in stony places; pound them with **vinegar** and should then drink of it, or mix it into his drink.

# Regimen of Life

Whoever cannot hold their urine and wets themselves <note>original: "vndersich feycht," literally "wets under himself."</note> at night should take **goat dung** <note>original: "geyß Dreck" or "geyß Bonen" (goat beans), a common folk-medical euphemism for droppings.</note> and burn it into a powder. When you wish to go to sleep at night, put as much of the powder into a cup or glass of fresh water as you can drink in one gulp. Do this for one night or three, and the condition will leave you.

<margin>Bowel movements.</margin>
Also, for one who cannot go to the stool, he should take four or five <term>lots</term> <note>A "lot" was a traditional German unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 15–16 grams.</note> of **senna**, and a little gall from a small fish—whichever kind you like—or **purging cassia** <note>original: "Cassie fistuli," the pods of the *Cassia fistula* tree, used as a laxative.</note> from the apothecary, or the bark of **turpeth** <note>original: "Turbit," the root of *Operculina turpethum*, a powerful purgative plant from the morning glory family.</note>, a <term>quintlin</term> <note>A "quintlin" or "Quentchen" was a small weight, about 3.75 grams or 1/4 of a lot.</note> of each, and two lots of crushed sugar. Put these together in a small box and eat a spoonful while fasting <note>original: "nüchtern," meaning on an empty stomach.</note> in a good soup broth. Then eat three spoonfuls of that same good broth and fast for three hours afterward; thus you will achieve a bowel movement.

<margin>Red flux.</margin>
Also, you should take **butter** <note>original: "Ancken," a dialect term for butter.</note> that was made in **May**, and eat it without salt once or thrice; thus the **red flux** <note>original: "rote rur," an old term for dysentery involving bloody stools.</note> will pass.

Or, boil **rose petals** in water and drink it; that stops the redness in the bowel movement.

<margin>Bowel movements.</margin>
Also, if you wish to produce a bowel movement whenever you like, take plenty of **poppy seeds** <note>original: "ölmagen," a regional term for poppy seeds.</note> and **peach kernels**—as many as you think is enough. Lay the peach kernels in a pewter dish and pour three spoonfuls of good wine over them. Cover it and let it stand overnight. In the morning, you should crush the kernels into the wine and add a little **alum** to it. When all this is done together and you take it in the morning while fasting, you will immediately go to the stool without harm.

<margin>Ficus-warts.</margin>
Also, take an **egg** and remove the white. Into the yolk remaining in the egg, push small cut pieces of all different colors of **miller’s cloth** <note>original: "Müllers thuchs." This likely refers to scraps of fabric used in milling or a specific type of coarse cloth.</note>. Cut another egg in two, place one shell over the first egg, and lay it in hot ash until it turns to powder. Keep this powder and sprinkle it upon the ailment <note>original: "schaden," referring here to the "Feigwarzen," which are genital warts or similar growths.</note>; you will recover.

<margin>Male weakness.</margin>
Also, if a man’s **secret parts** <note>original: "heimlich gemech," a common euphemism for genitals.</note> are so swollen that he cannot urinate, let him boil **mallow** <note>original: "Pappeln," which usually means poplars but in old herbals frequently refers to the mallow plant (*Malva*).</note> and **garlic** in wine until a third is boiled away. Then strain the wine through a clean cloth and drink from it. The swelling will retreat, and you will feel better.

If someone, whether man or woman, is very sick in their **secret parts** from scratching or otherwise, they should take **sage** and boil it well in wine. Afterward, the wine should be rubbed onto the ailment; it helps well.

<margin>For dizziness.</margin>
Also, take one and a half quintlin of **clove-pink powder** <note>original: "pulueris Dianthos," a restorative powder usually made from rosemary flowers or carnations.</note>, one quintlin each of the spice mixtures of **anise** and **cumin**, one quintlin of **galangal stomach tonic** <note>original: "dia galange Stomatici confortatiui," a medicinal paste or powder based on the root of the galangal plant.</note>, and one and a half quarters of **sugar**. Let this be made into a powder; used morning and evening, it drives away dizziness.

<margin>Stabbing pain on the right side.</margin>
Also, take seven ounces of **endive water**, half a pound of **rose water**, one quintlin each of **white and red sandalwood**, one <term>scruple</term> <note>A "scruple" (Scrupel) was a small apothecary weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</note> of **camphor**, and two lots of vinegar. Mix all of this together and moisten a cloth or a sponge in it. Lay it on the right side; it helps well for stabbing pains of the **liver**.

Take **rue** and **willow bark** in equal amounts; boil them in vinegar,

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions attributed to Marsilio Ficino, the influential 15th-century Italian scholar and physician. The previous page detailed remedies for urinary issues and digestive complaints. Here, the text transitions into topical treatments for side pain, distilled "waters" for hearing loss and headaches, and a complex herbal powder intended to preserve the senses of sight and hearing until death.</meta>

# Marsilio Ficino. 59

Soak a cloth, a sponge, or soft bread in this, and apply it warm to the side; it will greatly help you against the stinging in your left side.

### A good water for the hearing
<margin>A water for hearing.</margin>
A good water to use when a person does not hear well, provided the condition is new and has not lasted long. Take <gloss>for hearing</gloss> walnut-leaf water, wormwood water, and rosemary water—six <term>lots</term> <note>A "lot" was a unit of weight equal to about 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of each. Take eight lots of **ant eggs** <note>original: "Emsen eyer." Ant eggs (actually larvae/pupae) were a common ingredient in early modern ear remedies, believed to have "spirituous" qualities.</note> that are fresh and good. Put all of this into a glass, seal it well, and place it in **horse manure** <note>original: "Roßmist." This was a common method for providing slow, steady heat to a mixture to encourage fermentation or chemical breakdown.</note> or in a **water bath** <note>original: "Balneum Marie," literally "Mary’s Bath." A double-boiler method used in alchemy and pharmacy for gentle heating.</note> for eight days to <term>digest</term> and <term>putrefy</term>. Afterward, distill it in the water bath through an **alembic** <note>original: "Alembicum," a chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distilling liquids.</note>. Then place it in the sun for forty days, and then use it as needed.

### A water for deafness and headaches
Another good water: if you make it lukewarm and put it into the ears, it drives away the deafness of the ears that comes from **foul humors** <note>original: "böse feuchtigkeit," referring to the excess of bodily fluids—like phlegm—believed in Galenic medicine to cause illness.</note> of the head. And for one whose head aches badly, he should soak a double-folded linen cloth in it and lay it on his forehead for three or four days, and he will recover. This water also creates **sharp senses** and drives away unnatural heat. Take **vervain**, **rue** leaves, and **sage**—equal parts of each—and distill a water from them through an alembic; keep this for use.

### A restorative powder for sight and hearing
This is also a powder in which I have seen and performed great, wonderful effects to enlighten and clear the **eyesight**. It draws away the darkness and gloom of the eyes; it strengthens, empowers, and restores lost sight and hearing. This is for the sake of strengthening the head and the **organs** <note>original: "Instrument," meaning the physical structures of the eyes and ears.</note> in which the sight of the eyes and the hearing of the ears are located. It also has the power to preserve the strength of sight and hearing until a person reaches their natural death, if one uses it from time to time in the morning and evening—about the size of a small **chestnut**—on a slice of bread previously soaked in wine. Make the powder as follows: Take the blossoms of vervain (the ones with blue flowers), **euphrasia** <note>original: "Eufrasien," also known as Eyebright.</note> blossoms, and blue **chicory** blossoms <note>original: "Wegweiß blumen."</note>.

Also take **mallow** <note>original: "Sigmars kraut," likely *Malva alcea* or Hollyhock Mallow.</note>, which is shaped like the common mallow but with an upright stem, very much like the herb called **marshmallow** <note>original: "Ibisch," or *Althaea officinalis*.</note>, except the leaves are smaller than marshmallow; and take **bean blossoms**, three lots of each.

Also take **valerian** root—which cats like to eat—to sharpen the sight for seeing at night; the outer bark of chicory roots (the core in the middle having been thrown away) with the blue flowers, six lots; **anise** and **fennel seeds**, eight lots of each; **licorice** brought from Venice; and **juniper berries** that are perfectly ripe, two lots of each. Take **cinnamon stalks**, **cardamom**, and noble **red roses**, two lots of each. Take **Indian myrobalans**, **citrine myrobalans**, and **chebulic myrobalans** <note>The myrobalans (Mirabulani) were dried astringent fruits imported from India and the East, staple ingredients in medieval pharmacology for "purging" specific humors.</note>, two lots of each, plus the weight of nine barleycorns. All these pieces should be gathered between the **two feast days of Our Lady** <note>The period between the Assumption of Mary (August 15) and the Nativity of Mary (September 8), considered the holiest and most potent time for harvesting medicinal herbs.</note> when the moon is waxing. They should be medium-powdered so that they remain in the stomach longer.

<header>Rule of Life</header>

And when applied as described, it will dry the moisture of the eyes and reduce the redness of the eyes.

<margin>Eyes.</margin>
But another water, which drives away the darkness of the eyes—that is, when a person feels as though they are looking through a spiderweb or through a flame—if one puts a little into the eyes every morning and evening an hour before nightfall, it dispels it. Take **wormwood** <note>original: "Absinthij"</note> and **centaury** <note>original: "Centauree"</note>, five handfuls of each; half a pound of powdered **black cumin** <note>original: "Nigelle"</note>; two pounds of white wine. Crush them together and distill them through an **alembic** <note>original: "Alembicum," a distillation apparatus.</note> in a **water bath** <note>original: "Balneo Marie," literally "Mary’s Bath," a method of gentle heating using a double boiler.</note>; then it is prepared correctly.

<margin>Eyes.</margin>
A wonderful water from Master Peter of Spain <note>Petrus Hispanus, a 13th-century physician and philosopher who later became Pope John XXI; he was famous for his medical text "Thesaurus Pauperum" (Treasure of the Poor).</note>, mentioned previously, which clears the sight and is good for all spots in the eyes, strengthens and preserves the vision if one puts it in the eyes an hour before nightfall, and is made thus: Take in their season these herbs, as long as you can get them all green and fresh: fennel, rue, **eyebright** <note>original: "Euphrasien"</note>, endive, betony, red roses, **celandine** <note>original: "Schellwurz"</note>, and willow, in equal parts. Hack them all together while fresh, crush them, and soak them for a natural day <note>A "natural day" refers to a full 24-hour period.</note> in good white wine, so that it just barely covers them, in a water bath. Afterward, distill it through an alembic. The first water that comes out of it is as good as silver; the second as gold; the third as balsam.

<margin>Running eyes.</margin>
A water for when the eyes run due to heat, if one applies it an hour before nightfall: Take **mullein** <note>original: "Wullenblüet"</note> water, bean-blossom water, red and blue violet water, equal parts of each. Put them together in a glass and set it in the sun to purify itself; then it is prepared correctly.

A water for reddish eyelids, if one applies it every time: Take **marsh marigold** flowers <note>original: "Merzen blumen," literally "March flowers."</note>, otherwise called **monk's rhubarb** blossom water <note>original: "Pfaffen kraut blumen wasser."</note>, willow-leaf water, bean-blossom water, **silverweed** blossom water <note>original: "Genserich blumen wasser."</note>; mix equal parts together and set in the sun, used as stated above.

### On the beauty and clarity of the face.

First, a water which was used and found satisfactory by a Princess and Duchess of Austria to make a beautiful face. It drives away wrinkles, blemishes, and many ailments under the eyes and on the face, and makes black teeth white. But in truth, it is not as good for whitening teeth as what I will write hereafter, unless they were not black to begin with. It drives away foul, stinking breath and also makes the hair beautifully soft as silk, as well as gold-colored, and it stays that way. It drives away the lice and mites of the hair, and the hair turns gray very slowly if one wets a brush or comb in it and brushes or combs the hair every day, or every other day; do this for a month or two. Do the same for washing the face, and each time let it dry by itself. And this is made thus: Take **lily of the valley** <note>original: "Mayblumen"</note> alone without the leaves; **Solomon’s seal** <note>original: "Weißwurz"</note>, the root; peach blossoms; **lovage** <note>original: "Lobstöckel"</note>, the herb; **gold-root** <note>original: "Goldwurz," likely referring to turmeric or celandine</note>, the root thereof; **white bryony** <note>original: "Hundstkürbs," literally "dog-gourd."</note>, the root, which is called by another

named **peony** <note>original: "Peonia"</note> / **white lily** roots / two handfuls of each. Chop them small together and add seven *lot* <note>A traditional German unit of weight, roughly 1/2 ounce or 15 grams.</note> of **honey** that has been skimmed, prepared, and purified; **egg whites** that have been beaten and prepared from eight eggs; and as much white wine as needed. Beat, crush, and temper it all together with the herbs, flowers, and roots, which should be green and not dried. Place them for six days in a glass in **horse manure** <note>Used as a source of gentle, consistent heat in alchemy and early chemistry.</note> to **putrefy** <note>original: "Putrificieren," a process of breaking down organic matter through fermentation or gentle heat.</note>; and on the seventh day, it shall be distilled three times through an **alembic** <note>original: "Alembicum," a distillation apparatus.</note> in a **water bath** <note>original: "Balneo Marie."</note>. Pour it back over the **dregs** <note>original: "feces," referring to the solid residue left after distillation.</note> just as often. The first time, it is clear as silver; the second time, it is like an **oil** <note>original: "Oleum."</note>; and the third time, it is yellow-gold like **gold**. Afterward, one should hang a quintal of **camphor**, powdered and prepared as has often been taught, into it. Then the water is correct and good for the aforementioned matters. Therefore, to protect the face from such afflictions, one should wash the face with it at times and let it dry on its own each time; however, one should not go out much into the air during that time, as the person will gain a delicate, young skin.

<margin>Eruptions on the face.</margin>
A water that restores the lost color to the face if one drinks it often; and this is made thus: Take **betony** water, **rosemary** water, **verbena** water, **polypody** <note>original: "Engelsüß," literally "Angel-sweet," refers to the fern *Polypodium vulgare*.</note> water, and **wormwood** water, mixed in equal parts and set in the sun, and used as stated above.

A righteous water which is good for those with an "erupting" red face, called ***Gutta rosacea*** <note>Rosacea; a chronic inflammatory skin condition.</note>; it also extinguishes all evil heat from under the face. And it shall be made thus: One shall take **nightshade**, **knotgrass** <note>original: "Wegdritt."</note>, roots, **bryony** roots <note>original: "Hundeskürbs," though the text notes it is also called "Peonia," botanically these are usually distinct plants—Bryony and Peony.</note>, and **bean blossoms**, a handful of each; half a *lot* of **camphor** (the camphor should be powdered beforehand), and then everything is crushed together, chopped, and distilled by an **alembic** in a **water bath** at least twice; applied with a small cloth, it helps.

A water that drives away the **pustules** <note>original: "Bläterlin," small blisters or pimples.</note> from the face and the eyes: Take **nettles**, crushed small and soaked in wine for four days, and then distilled by an **alembic**. Take equal parts of that water and **pennyroyal** <note>original: "Boley wasser."</note> water; soak a small cloth in it and lay it over the pustules; they will soon vanish. Or take **bean blossom** water and **egg whites** in equal parts, distill a little from it, and set it in the sun for eight days; then put a little camphor into it and use it; it helps.

<margin>Fissures.</margin>
A water that is good for **fissures** <note>original: "Risemen," referring to cracks, chapping, or deep wrinkles in the skin.</note> of the face. Take **goat milk** water, **white lily** water, **wild zedoary** <note>original: "wilder Zitwan," a root similar to ginger.</note> root water, and half a pound of **vine water** that drips from the vines <note>Sap collected from pruned grapevines in spring.</note>; half a pound of burned **tartar** <note>original: "Weinstein."</note>; green **arum** roots <note>original: "Aaron wurzlen," also known as Cuckoo-pint.</note>; and **Solomon's seal** <note>original: "Weißwurzlen."</note>; four *lot* of each. Crush them together and **digest** <note>original: "Digerier," to soften or break down using gentle heat.</note> it for eight days; afterward, distill it by an **alembic**, and then it is prepared correctly.

# Marsilio Ficino. LXI

Walnut **Rob** <note>A "rob" is a fruit juice thickened by boiling with sugar or honey.</note> / two **lot** <note>A traditional unit of weight, approximately 15 grams.</note> each / **Cold tragacanth tablets** <note>original: "Dia dragantum frigidum in tabulis," a cooling medicinal confection made with gum tragacanth.</note> / **Gum Arabic** / one lot each / This shall be distilled **using an alembic in a water bath** <note>original: "per Alembicum in Balneo Marię," a standard chemical method for gentle heating.</note> for the second time / each time poured over the **residue** <note>original: "Feces," the solid dregs left after distillation.</note> / this should be used to gargle in the throat.

A noble water / that is good for teeth falling out / those that are loose and shaky / and also when the gums have become loose and soft / if one holds this in the mouth for a long time / it draws the excess moisture to itself / and the gums will stand firm again as before. The water shall be made thus. Take dried red roses / pomegranate blossoms / called **Balustia** / the bark of a wood called *Porß* / its other name is **Tamarisk bark** <note>original: "Cortex Tamarisci."</note> / half a lot each / **Acacia**, which is sloe juice / dried **pellitory** <note>original: "Piretrum," a root used since antiquity to stimulate saliva and treat toothaches.</note> / **mastic** <note>A resin from the Pistacia lentiscus tree used for dental hygiene.</note> / one **quintlin** <note>A small weight, about 3.5 to 4 grams.</note> each / common alum one lot / distilled unripe sloe water / wild pear water that is not yet distilled / medlar water that is not quite ripe / distilled unripe quince water / eight lot each. The ingredients are to be crushed coarsely / and not to the finest powder / then the waters are poured on / placed in a **cucurbit** <note>A gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation.</note> / and a "blind head" <note>A distillation cap without a spout, used to circulate vapors without collecting them yet.</note> placed upon it / then allowed to **decay** <note>original: "geputrificiert," referring to a process of steeping or gentle fermentation to break down the organic matter.</note> in a **water bath** for fourteen days / and distilled **using an alembic in a "horse’s belly"** <note>original: "in ventre Equino," a method where the heat is provided by a bed of fermenting horse manure, which provides a steady, low temperature.</note> / and let this be done a third time. But if one cannot obtain this water quickly / or the person is so poor / let there be taken in place of the water an equal weight of **verjuice** <note>original: "Agrest," the highly acidic juice of unripe grapes.</note> from sour grapes / or the harshest red wine you can find.

<margin>To make teeth white.</margin>
Here is a little secret included / which will be dearer to some than ten Guilders / this and many more proven secrets are to be found in this little book. You should take rosemary stalks / burn them gently / until they become black coals / and then take them in a clean little cloth / rub the teeth with it / for the first one or three days / thereafter barely once every fourteen days or longer / and they will become white / even if they were yellow before.

<margin>for shortness of breath.</margin>

Take blue violet herb water, mallow leaf water / six lot each / purslane water / borage water / four lot each / a handful of the bark of **Barberry** <note>original: "Jelenger," often referring to Barberry or similar bitter barks.</note> / preferably with the herb or stalks / cut small / **poppy confection** <note>original: "Dia Papauer."</note> / three lot / **Syrup of Violets** / two lot / This shall be **digested** <note>original: "Gedigeriert," a process of steeping ingredients in a warm liquid.</note> day and night in a **water bath** / and then distilled in a **water bath** twice / thereafter consumed and used in the aforementioned measure / it helps.

<margin>for indigestion and a sour stomach. Jaundice.</margin>
A noble water / which is not expensive in money / but very precious and useful in its effect. For it is good for a bad, slow stomach / for gluttony and belching of the stomach / if one drinks it morning and evening / three or four lot each time / and used thus / it brings back the desire and craving for food. It is also good for **jaundice** <note>original: "Gilbe" and "vbergeel," literally "the yellow" or "over-yellowing."</note> / and for heat of the liver / also in hot fevers / drunk as mentioned above / and it is to be made thus. # IXI
->Regiment des Lebens<-
<note>The Rule of Life</note>

...chopped small / add just as much **barberry** <note>original: "Saurachberlin," also known as "Erbsal."</note> / both of these pounded together and distilled **in a horse’s belly** <note>original: "in Ventre equino," a method of providing steady, gentle heat by burying a sealed vessel in fermenting horse manure.</note> using an **alembic** / and then set in the sun in a well-sealed glass for one month / Then use it as instructed / and this is a noble water for the poor who do not have much money.

<margin>Belching of the stomach.</margin>
A water that strengthens and maintains the stomach in health / also for over-indulgence, vomiting / and belching of the stomach / Take seven **lot** <note>A traditional unit of weight, approx. 15 grams.</note> of **quince** water / wormwood and mint water / six lot of each / four lot of **centaury** water / **elecampane** root and **betony** water / two lot of each / four lot of **sweet flag** <note>original: "Calami Aromatici."</note> / two lot of the thin skins of **chicken gizzards** / one lot of **cuckoo pint** <note>original: "Aaron wurtzel."</note> root / Whatever is to be powdered shall be powdered / **putrefied** <note>In early medicine, this refers to a controlled fermentation or aging of the ingredients.</note> and distilled for the third time in a **water bath** <note>original: "in Balneo Marie."</note> / and taken internally, it helps as stated above.

<margin>For the trembling of the heart.</margin>
A noble water for the pounding / quaking / and trembling of the heart / Take twenty-four lot of **bugloss** <note>original: "Ochsen zungen," or borage.</note> water / seven lot of **lemon balm** water / borage and linden blossom water / six lot of each / Put these all together in a silver vessel / and quench **glowing gold** in it / until a fourth part is consumed / then add this recipe to it / if the person’s urine tends toward red: one lot of **coral-based medicinal powder** <note>original: "Species diacotali" (likely "diacoralli").</note> prepared expertly / half a lot each of **electuary of gemstones** and **pearl-based medicine** <note>original: "Species Electuarium de Gemmis / Diamargariton."</note> / one **quint** <note>A small weight, roughly 4 grams.</note> of **spodium** <note>Ash produced by burning bone or ivory, used to cool the body.</note> / half a quint of the **bone from the heart of a cleaned stag** <note>original: "Os de corde Cerui Mundati." The "stag's heart bone" is a piece of hardened cartilage found in the hearts of older deer, once highly prized as a cardiac tonic.</note> / one quint each of **white and red behen** <note>Traditional medicinal roots (Centaurea behen and Statice limonium).</note> / and **citron peel**. It shall be putrefied and **digested** <note>Aged in gentle heat.</note> in horse manure for eight days / and then distilled in a water bath using an alembic. But if the sick person’s urine tends toward white or coldness / the **coral-based powder** and the **electuary of gemstones** should be left out / and in their place use **aloe-wood medicine** <note>original: "Dialignum Aloes."</note> / and **sweet musk medicine according to Mesue** <note>original: "Dia musci dulcis Secundum Mesue," referring to the recipes of the famous Persian physician Mesue the Younger.</note> / each according to its weight as mentioned above.

<margin>Against stabbing pains around the heart.</margin>
Another good water for common poor folk / if one uses it in the morning and evening / eight lot each time / it is good if a person has a stabbing or aching around the heart / and it is made thus. Take eight lot each of **bugloss**, **borage** flowers, and **lily of the valley** water / four lot each of **endive** water, **blue violets**, and **wild sage** water / These shall be mixed together and set in the sun for thirty days / if it can be done at the time of use.

<margin>For all abscesses.</margin>
A water good for all **abscesses** <note>original: "Apostemen," referring to inflammatory swellings, boils, or internal tumors.</note> / also for illnesses in the body / and all fainting / If one wishes to help him / take of the water as described hereafter / half an eggshell full / drink this morning and evening / and also just as much wine each time. Take **scabious** <note>original: "Apostemen kraut," literally "abscess-herb."</note> / **field thistle** / **sage** / **lemon balm** / **comfrey** <note>original: "Schofwurtz," also called "Garthage."</note> / **rue** / **chickweed** <note>original: "Hünerköl."</note> / **wormwood** / **hyssop** / **lavender** flowers / **endive** / **sanicle** <note>original: "Rotebuck."</note> / **borage** flowers / an equal amount of these eight herbs, one handful of each / all chopped small and put into a **can** <note>original: "Kanthe," a large pitcher or jug.</note> / that holds two **measures** <note>original: "Maß," a volume approximately equal to a liter.</note> or into a new glazed pot / and pour two measures of good wine over it / seal it well / and bury it in a cellar under the earth

...and let it stand for fourteen days; after that, add the **spices** <note>original: "Species"</note>: **mace** <note>original: "Muscatblüht"</note>, **nutmeg**, and **cinnamon quills** / four **lot** <note>A lot is a traditional unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of each. These should be powdered / then place it in a **water bath** <note>original: "Balneum Mariae," a vessel placed in boiling water to provide gentle, even heat.</note> / and distill it by an **alembic** <note>A lid or hood used in distillation to collect and cool vapors.</note> / after that, place it in a glass, well-sealed, in the sun / for one or two months until it is ready for use.

A good water to make for an old, moist **cough** <note>original: "Husten"</note> / Take hyssop water / pennyroyal water / **maidenhair fern** <note>original: "Capilli Veneris"</note> water / and horehound water / half a pound of each / **Iris compound** <note>original: "Dia Jris," an electuary made from Orris root.</note> according to the **Antidotarium Nicolai** <note>original: "secundum Nicolaum," referring to a standard medieval medical text.</note> / **Calamint compound** <note>original: "Dia Calamentum"</note> / three lot of each / **Digest** <note>original: "Digerier," meaning to steep in gentle heat.</note> and distill this by an alembic / and use it as written above.

<margin>Cough in children.</margin>
Another water for young children for the cough / of which they should be given a small spoonful to drink every time / Take violet and pumpkin water / six lot of each / **Tragacanth compound** <note>original: "Dia dragantum"</note> three lot / Distill this by an alembic in a water bath.

<margin>Rotting of the lungs.</margin>
A common water / that one drinks if their **lung is rotting** <note>referring to pulmonary decay or severe infection/tuberculosis</note> / three times every day as stated above / it helps / and it is made thus / take oak-leaf water / hyssop water / blue **iris** <note>original: "Gilgen"</note> root and **toadflax** <note>original: "Lienen blumen"</note> water / maidenhair fern water / Put all of this into a glass / seal it well / and then set it in the sun / then it is prepared correctly.

<margin>For thirst.</margin>
Likewise, a laudable water for thirst / which is pleasant to use / whether one takes two lot of it on its own / or mixes it into another drink / and drinks from it / it is made thus / Take **sow-thistle** <note>original: "Genßdistel"</note> water / violet water / sorrel and liverwort water / four lot of each / wine made of pomegranates / six lot / lemon juice / three lot / orange juice / three lot / **lemon syrup** or **simple oxysaccharum** <note>original: "Syropus de limonis vel Osisacra simplex," a medicinal syrup of vinegar and sugar.</note> according to Nicolaus / **Citrus-sorrel syrup** <note>original: "Syropus de Acetose citrini"</note> / three lot of each / red **sandalwood** / one lot / **Tabarzet sugar** <note>original: "Zuccar taberzet," a high-quality refined white sugar.</note> six **quintlin** <note>A small weight, about 4 grams.</note>. Whatever is to be powdered should be powdered / and all of this put together and **digested** in a water bath / for one natural day, which is twenty-four hours / after that, distill it again in a water bath / then pour it back over the **dregs** <note>original: "feces," the solid remains after distillation.</note> / and for a second time digest and distill it as before / let this be done for a third time / then it is prepared correctly.

<margin>For all heat of the liver.</margin>
A good water for all heat of the liver / and for all unnatural heat of the whole body / especially if one drinks it / or also soaks cloths or **tow** <note>original: "Werck," coarse flax or hemp fibers used for compresses.</note> in it / and lays it upon the liver. Take sow-thistle / six handfuls / **chicory** <note>original: "Wegweiß"</note> three handfuls / maidenhair fern / liverwort / nightshade / **maidenhair** <note>original: "Adianthos," the Greek name for the fern.</note>. Crush all of these together / and distill it by an alembic / Also mix this water with the wine that you drink / it helps.

A noble water for when a person **urinates in drops** <note>original: "tröpflecht harnet," referring to strangury or urinary retention.</note> / or has **painful urination** <note>original: "Harnwind"</note> / or **cold urine** <note>original: "kalten Seych," likely referring to incontinence or a lack of "vital heat" in the bladder.</note> / so that one gives him this water to drink in the morning / at midday, and in the evening / three or four lot each time / And he should also **bathe** <note>original: "bähen," to apply warm vapors or fomentation.</note> himself over a hot stone / upon which wine is poured while it is hot / in which wine **wild caraway** <note>original: "Mathkümel"</note> has been boiled. But the water is made thus / Take parsley water / **smallage** <note>original: "Epff," wild celery.</note> water / chervil water / knotgrass water / one lot of each / horseradish /

# Regimen of Life
<meta>catchword: Item</meta>

**Radish** and **garlic water** / eight **lot** <note>A lot is a traditional unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of each / **madder** <note>original: "Rubetinctorum," a plant used for red dye and medicine.</note> four lot / **bittersweet berries** <note>original: "Schluttenber"</note> / **bindweed seeds** / two lot of each / crush these coarsely / and pour the waters over them / and let it **putrefy** <note>In alchemy and early pharmacy, "putrefaction" refers to letting a substance sit in gentle heat, like horse manure, to break it down.</note> for eight days in **horse manure** / then **distill** it by an **alembic** <note>A lid or hood used in distillation to collect and cool vapors.</note> in a **water bath** <note>original: "Balneo Marie"</note> three times / and each time pour it back over the **dregs** <note>original: "Feces," the solid remains after distillation.</note> / then it is ready.

<margin>If a man has soreness on his member.</margin>
If a man’s **penis** <note>original: "rut"</note> is sore and painful between the skin and the head / let him soak clean cloths in the following water / and having washed it, place the cloth in between / it helps. Take **red corn-poppy** water / **long birthwort** water / **privet leaf** water / seven lot of each / **mullein** water / **walnut leaf** water / seven lot of each / mixed together and set in the sun for forty days to purify itself.

<margin>For the pestilence.</margin>
A water for the **Pestilenz** <note>The Plague.</note> from a **Turkish doctor**. Take **walnuts** around **St. John’s Day** <note>June 24th, midsummer.</note> / one hundred in number / green with the outer husks still on / and cut or crush them into small pieces / and pour good vinegar over them / **scabious**, **verbena**, **nightshade**, **houseleek** / one handful of each. Cut them with a water / and then add the nuts with the vinegar / boil it / and let it stand overnight / After that, distill it by an alembic with a gentle fire / keep that for when it is needed / and then give the sick person a spoonful.

<margin>pestilence.</margin>
Another water good for the pestilence / from the **Count Palatine’s doctor**. You should take one **measure** <note>original: "maß," a liquid unit often around 1 to 1.5 liters.</note> of **goat’s milk** / two handfuls of **fumitory** <note>original: "Taubenkropff"</note> / well-crushed and soaked together for eight days / then distilled by an alembic / give this water to a person when the pestilence comes upon them before they sleep / and if it comes with heat <note>fever</note> / give them one spoonful / but if it comes with cold <note>chills</note> / give them two spoonfuls / it drives it away.

<margin>Hardening iron.</margin>
A water and how one should distill it / in which one **hardens iron** and makes it durable / Take the herb **knotgrass** <note>original: "Wegweiß"</note> / **motherwort** / **cuckoo-pint** <note>original: "Aaronkraut"</note> / **jewelweed** <note>original: "Springwurz"</note> / **celandine** / **ragwort** / **violet** herb / **vervain** <note>original: "Eisenkraut"</note> / **fern** / **stinking chamomile** / called **toad-herb** / **fennel** herb / **coriander** herb / taken in equal parts when they are in bloom / from these distill a water by an alembic / and in this **quench** the iron like so. Make the iron warm / and sprinkle it a little with the water / then make it warm again / do this three times / after that, make it red-hot and quench it in the water slowly / this has been **proven** <note>original: "geprobirt," indicating the recipe was tested and found effective.</note>.

<margin>Headache.</margin>
For one whose head aches so much from **congestion** <note>original: "verstopffung," referring to a blockage of humors or phlegm.</note> / that you cannot breathe well / then buy one or two lot of good **cinnamon quills** / and eat a little of it / or if it were powdered, sprinkle it on a slice of bread soaked in wine / that takes away the congestion / and lessens the **phlegm** / from which the congestion comes.

<margin>Quenching thirst.</margin>
If a person has a great thirst / and is not permitted to drink enough / one should quench the thirst thus / Take **pebbles** / and lay them in cold water / they should be the size of a walnut / then put one in the mouth from time to time / it helps.

Also, take **toasted bread** with **salt** and **vinegar** / crush it and make it into a thick **plaster** / lay that upon the **stomach** and **mouth** / that is, upon the **pit of the stomach** <note>original: "Hertzgrüblin." Literally "heart-pit," referring to the epigastrium or the area just below the ribs.</note> / and if you rub the sick person's mouth with it / it strengthens them well and gives them power.

Also, the water in which **quince seeds** have lain / takes away the **heaving of the stomach** and **vomiting** / for it strengthens the stomach and makes for good digestion. If it is the right season / one may boil **quinces** alone into a <term>electuary</term> <note>original: "Latwergen." For **vomiting** / Take one **lot** <note>A unit of weight, approximately 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of powdered <term>betony</term> <note>original: "Bethonica." A common medicinal herb in the mint family used for various ailments.</note> / add to it four lot of **honey** / and make an **electuary** from it / give that to the sick person every day in the morning on an empty stomach. **Almansor** <note>The Latinized name for Rhazes, a Persian polymath and influential physician.</note> says / if **medlars** <note>original: "Nespelen." A fruit resembling a small brown apple, eaten when overripe.</note> are eaten, they stop vomiting from coming up / Therefore **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar.</note> says / They strengthen the stomach and take away the heaving and nausea.

### For the cough and thirst / also roughness of the throat.

You should take a **measure of water** / add two lot of **sugar** to it / boil it down to a third / then strain it through a cloth / and let it become cool / Then you may drink as much of it as you wish. Also, it is good that you wash your **mouth** in the morning and at midday with **warm water** / and rub your **teeth** well / also wash the **forehead** and **temples** with warm water / that cleanses and makes the **head** light.

Also, if you would gladly be rid of the **catarrh** <note>original: "fluß." Literally "the flux" or "the flow," referring here to excess mucus or rheum.</note> / then take nine **sulfur matches** <note>original: "Schwefel kerglin." Small bits or candles of sulfur.</note> / put them in a pot / and light them / hang a **cloak** over your head / open your mouth wide / and hold yourself over the pot / let the **smoke** <note>original: "laum." Vapors or fumes.</note> thus go into you / do this morning and evening / so it will check the catarrh.

<margin>Pain in the sides.</margin>
Also, for whoever has pain in the **sides** / let him take **linseed cakes** / boil them in water / then soak a linen cloth therein / and lay it thus warm upon the side / so it will pass from you at once. When a person has pain in the sides / especially in the **left side** / then take a measure of **white wine** / a sixth part of a measure of **honey** / a handful of <term>parsley</term> <note>original: "Peterlinkraut."</note> / or four lot of its **seed** / boil that in a new pot / until a sixth part remains / and give that to the sick person to drink / when it is cold / morning and evening / he will surely recover.

Or if a person has great **stabbing** and **aching** in the sides / I have taken half a pound of **milk** / four lot of **honey** / two lot of **wheat flour** / a half-creutzer's worth of <term>saffron</term> / applied warm / as I have written above / it helps from that hour on.

LXII Regiment of Life

...or it comes to him with great harm / help him thus / as follows. Take **nettle root** with the herb / chop it small and boil it very well in **water** / until it becomes soft / then put it in a small white **sack** / and lay it upon [the area] / while it is warm / do this three times a day / and warm it again in the first broth / thus it remains inside afterward. Or bake the herb for him in a **small cake** / and give it to him to eat in a **soup** / so the bowel remains in the body without harm / and it is also well proven that it is true / that same herb has small red flowers / one should give it to him to eat for eight days. But if you cannot get the herb / then take twelve handfuls of **oak bark meal** <note>original: "Lorinden melh." Flour made from the bark of oak trees, rich in tannins and used as an astringent.</note> / that is / meal from **oak bark** / put it in a small sack / boil it as before / and sit upon it / so it [the bowel] goes back in.

<margin>
Dysentery.
<note>original: "Rotstulgang." Literally "red stool-going," referring to bloody diarrhea or dysentery.</note>
</margin>
For **dysentery** / Take half a **lot** <note>A unit of weight, approximately 7.5 to 8 grams.</note> of **dragon’s blood** <note>original: "sanguis Draconis." A bright red resin obtained from certain plants, highly valued in the medieval period for its ability to stop bleeding.</note> in powder / drink it in a little **broth** / or eat it in a **soft-boiled egg** / that is proven for binding [the bowels]. Or give him **common plantain** <note>original: "Weggraß." Literally "way-grass."</note> to eat in a **soup** / or in a **mash** / and however he may eat it / it stops the blood below and above / or bake it in an **egg** / as one bakes a soft omelet <note>original: "habich." A dialect term for a simple, soft-cooked egg dish.</note> so that it stays soft.

### A true art for stopping the
### bloody stool.

Also take **walnut catkins** <note>original: "Nußbaum zapffen." The long, drooping flower clusters of the walnut tree.</note> as they first fall from the tree / and **hazelnut catkins** / put them in a basin / and dry them in the **sun** or in the **oven** / so that they do not rot / pound them into a clean **powder** / And whoever has the **bloody flux** <note>original: "Blutrör." A historical term for severe intestinal bleeding or dysentery.</note> / give him a **quintlin** <note>A small weight, roughly 3.75 grams.</note> in a baked **egg** with **oil** so that it is hard / take this for three mornings on an empty stomach / each time the same amount / but if it will not stop / then give him a half **lot** of the powder in an egg.

<margin>Bowel movements.</margin>
An art for the bowel movement. Boil **cabbage** / let it boil for **fourteen hours** without ceasing / and keep it well covered at all times. Take a portion of the herb as large as a man's head / put it in a pot that holds three **measures** <note>original: "maß." A standard liquid measure, though the exact volume varied by region.</note> of **water** / cut it small / set it on a **trivet** / boil it well without ceasing so that it does not overflow / if the broth decreases / then add more water to it / though it is better boiled from one broth / it binds [the bowels] / And for one who has had the "running out" <note>Diarrhea.</note> for so long / that one fears he may die / then take the boiled cabbage / make a **pap** <note>original: "Müßlin." A soft, porridge-like food given to the sick.</note> from it with **milk** / which has been boiled very well beforehand / lest it overflow / he may eat this whenever he wants / but it is best on an empty stomach / or make him a pap from **crayfish shells** / which have been softened in milk / and take them out / pound and cook them in the first milk again / and give him that to eat / and upon the pap a **quintlin** / of **dragon's blood** / that binds well.

<margin>Cannot urinate.</margin>
Also if a person cannot **pass water** / or if no urine at all can come from the person / then you should take a **new brick** that has never yet been wetted / and make the brick glowing hot / and boil **meadow cumin** <note>original: "Mathkümbel." Likely wild caraway or meadow cumin, known for its diuretic properties.</note> in good **wine** / pour the wine with the meadow cumin onto the glowing brick / set it under you / and let the **steam** go well into you / so the urine comes back because of the warmth.

# Marsilio Ficino

...again. Or take **watercress** boiled with **wine** in a new pot / and sit over it [the steam] as warm as you can endure / that promotes urination well and is effective.

<margin>Urinary blockage.</margin>
If a male person develops the "cold piss" / that is / <term>strangury</term> <note>original: "Strangurea." A condition where urination is painful, frequent, and slow, often occurring drop by drop.</note> or the **urinary blockage** / smear his navel with nothing more than warm **tallow** <note>original: "Unschlit." Rendered animal fat used for ointments and candles.</note> / and it will soon pass for you.

If you wish to break the **urinary stone** quickly / so that it will pass / then take **goat’s blood** / put it in a glass / set it in the sun [to dry] / powder it finely / and take half a **lot** <note>A unit of weight, approximately 7.5 to 8 grams.</note> of the powder every night with **wine** to drink / when you wish to go to sleep / thus the stone breaks so that it will pass without harm / and as it is about to leave you / you should eat **parsley seeds** and **gromwell** <note>original: "Mörhirß." Likely Lithospermum officinale; in the medieval "Doctrine of Signatures," its stone-like seeds suggested it could treat kidney stones.</note> finely powdered on a slice of bread / in the morning and evening when you go to sleep / and wet the bread beforehand in wine / then the stone will grow no more / and will not get larger / because it destroys it / so that it cannot stay together. One may also use **hare’s blood** in the same way / it also drives out the stone / and it would be especially good if one drowned the hare in **vinegar**.

Item: **Angelica water** drunk every morning in the amount of 2 lots / is strengthening to the body.

Item: **Burnet-saxifrage water** <note>original: "Bibenel wasser." Pimpinella saxifraga, traditionally used as a plague preventative.</note> drunk in the amount of 4 lots / then one is safe from accidental illness <note>Sudden-onset diseases, often referring to the plague.</note> for that same day.

Item: **Gentian water** drunk at times in the amount of three lots / that lengthens one's life.

Item: **Feverfew water** <note>original: "Mutterkraut wasser."</note> used / and the hair brushed with it / prevents it from soon turning gray. Item: **Rosemary water** drunk / morning and evening in the amount of one and a half lots / or mixed into one’s drink / and the body smeared with it / that gladdens the bodily spirits / and delays aging for a long time.

->**End of the Regimen of Mar-**<-
->**silio Ficino.**<-

<meta>This page continues a theological reflection on the trial of Christ, specifically focusing on the legal injustices he faced compared to the mercy he showed others. It transitions into a discussion of the "Woman Taken in Adultery" as a symbolic parallel.</meta>

# How Dishonestly Christ was Brought Forth for Judgment

...would be hindered. For he was more eager for his own death out of love—even for his enemies—than his enemies were out of envy. Secondly, the Jews did not want the Lord to be questioned or heard, for they feared that if he were to defend himself—which he was immeasurably capable of doing—their plots and malice would fail, etc. And although it was a great outrage against Christ (to whom the Father has given all judgment, even according to his humanity, <gloss>John, chapter 5</gloss>, <note>original: "Jo. am. v. ca."</note> just as he possesses it by right from eternity according to his divinity), that he should have been vindicated and summoned and questioned according to the forms of the law as to whether he had done this or that of which he was accused—which is the point of this section—yet the outrage of the Jews was even more shameful and dishonorable. They desired that the Lord be condemned to death unheard, based solely on their words (which were nonetheless obvious lies). Yet Christ willed to suffer both, so that his very suffering might come to our aid when we come before his strict, anxious, and terrifying judgment. In that judgment, a person being questioned cannot answer even one thing out of a thousand; there we must give an account of all our words and thoughts, though we cannot even account for a tenth part of our works. For this reason, Christ chose to be questioned and to remain silent, just like a guilty man who cannot defend himself,

<column-break/>

so that his silence might speak for us in the strict judgment of his justice.

¶ In this sixty-fourth <term>figure<note>A "figure" (German: *Figur*) in this context refers to a specific illustration or a symbolic scene in the manuscript's narrative cycle.</note></term>, the Lord protects the poor female sinner who was caught in adultery and was to be stoned according to the law. She was brought before him by the <term>hypocrites<note>The original German uses "Gleißner," a common term for religious hypocrites, particularly referring to the Pharisees in this context.</note></term> so that he might judge what should be done with her. They did this to test him, waiting to see what he would say, so that they might accuse, slander, and rebuke him either for transgressing and contradicting the divine law, or else for a lack of gentleness, for hardness, and for mercilessness against his own teaching. But how he put them to shame in their own consciences and delivered the poor woman from their ambush and assured her against damnation—that is recorded in the Holy Gospel of <gloss>John, chapter 8.</gloss> And who could fail to notice in what great shame and fear the poor woman stood there before the Lord, in front of all the people—of whom there were many at the Lord's preaching (as Saint John reports)—and before the haughty hypocrites, her accusers. But the gentle, sweet heart of Christ could not long endure her shame and trembling, so he reassured her against her terror and comforted her in her sorrow. And this figure belongs to the first point of the 54th <term>objection<note>The term "gegenwurff" (objection or counter-argument) suggests this text follows a structured theological debate or a series of meditations on the life of Christ.</note></term>.

<summary>This page contrasts the illegal and silent submission of Christ during his own trial with the merciful legal defense he provided for the woman caught in adultery. It argues that Christ's silence at his trial serves as a legal "plea" for mercy on behalf of humanity at the Final Judgment.</summary>

<keywords>Trial of Christ, Mercy, Justice, Adultery, Hypocrites, Gospel of John, Final Judgment, Legal procedure</keywords>
